Do we need to do 0.0000000001 visibility? How do we acoomplish this such that the smoke sprite's visuals don't override the fill texture's visuals?

No — don’t use “0.0000000001 visibility.” The clean approach is:

Keep the smoke sprite invisible so it never renders to the screen.
Use a separate hidden mask image (maskImg) built from the smoke sprite’s texture + current frame to do the bitmap masking of the fill.
That way the mask follows the animation, but the smoke’s pixels never draw on top of the fill.
This already matches the mask‑image path we’re using: the fill sprite renders, the mask image crops it, and the smoke sprite itself stays invisible.

If we ever switch back to “native sprite mask,” then the mask sprite must be visible for masking to work (Phaser uses its rendered alpha). In that case you’d have to keep it visible but at 0 alpha, which is fragile. So the correct solution for your goal is maskImg path + invisible smoke.

If you want me to force this behavior globally (i.e., always use maskImg for effect masks and never use native sprite masks), say the word and I’ll lock it down.

Got it — and your correction matters:

* Your **border animating correctly** proves the smoke sprite exists, is updating frames, and its alpha is changing.
* The fact that the **fill does not match that border** means one thing: **the fill is not being rendered through the same mask path** (even if you *think* you “set a mask”).

There are a handful of *very specific* Phaser reasons this happens.

## First: “non-square filled texture” isn’t a thing in Phaser bounds

Every GameObject still draws a **rectangle** (quad). The “non-square shape” is purely from **alpha in the texture**. So yes: you can *visually* have a non-square fill, but its “bounds” are always rectangular.

And when you combine:

* fill alpha (your irregular waves image)
  with
* smoke alpha (your animated mask)

Phaser does:
**finalAlpha = fillAlpha × smokeMaskAlpha**
So the fill can be “not square” **and** still be masked — that part is fine.

So the problem isn’t “non-square.” The problem is: **your mask isn’t affecting the fill draw call you’re looking at.**

---

## Why your border can animate perfectly while the fill ignores it

Here are the 4 real-world causes I see for exactly your symptom set:

### 1) You accidentally have **two fills**

* One masked (working, but maybe behind / subtle)
* One unmasked (on top) — the “gigantic doing nothing” one you’re seeing

**Fast test:** temporarily do:
js
fill.setVisible(false);
If you *still* see the big waves, you’re looking at a different object than the one you masked.

### 2) The fill is using a **custom pipeline / shader**

This is the big one when people say “I can see my mask sprite animating, but it won’t mask my effect.”

If your fill has something like:
js
fill.setPipeline('SomePipeline');
then masking may not apply, because Phaser’s mask system is implemented in the standard WebGL pipeline path. A custom pipeline can bypass/ignore the mask pass unless you explicitly support it.

**Fast test:** remove the pipeline for 30 seconds.
js
fill.resetPipeline();
fill.clearFX?.();
If masking suddenly works: that’s your answer.

**Fix when you need the shader:**
Render your shader output into a **RenderTexture** (or a normal Sprite that uses the default pipeline), then mask *that* result.

### 3) The mask is being created from the smoke sprite, but the smoke sprite is not in the same **camera/filter path**

If you’re doing cameras, UI cameras, minimap cameras, etc., it’s very easy for the mask sprite to be visible in one camera and the fill to be in another. Your border could be drawn in the “right” camera, while the fill is rendered in a different camera pass.

**Hard fix:** force them to share camera visibility:
js
maskSprite.cameraFilter = fill.cameraFilter;
### 4) You applied the mask to the wrong thing (Container gotcha)

If the fill lives in a Container and you mask the Container or a sibling, it won’t do what you think. Apply the mask to the **exact object that is drawing the waves**.

**Fast truth check:**
js
console.log('fill has mask?', !!fill.mask);
---

## The one diagnostic that cuts through all of this

Do this *after* you think you’ve masked the fill:
js
// move the MASK far away
maskSprite.x += 1000;
**Expected if masking is truly applied:** the fill should basically disappear (or only a weird sliver remains), because the visible window moved away.

**If nothing changes:** the fill you’re seeing is **not masked** (wrong object, wrong pipeline, wrong camera, or you have a second unmasked fill on top).

---

## The “works even with weird textures” canonical setup

This is the simplest “I don’t care how huge the texture is, just mask it” version:
js
const fill = this.add.image(x, y, 'waves2');     // huge is fine
const smoke = this.add.sprite(x, y, 'smoke', 0);
smoke.play('smokeAnim');

// keep smoke renderable (don’t visible=false while debugging)
smoke.setAlpha(1);

fill.setMask(smoke.createBitmapMask());

// sanity checks
console.log('fill has mask?', !!fill.mask);
If *that* doesn’t work, you are in one of:

* Canvas renderer
* custom pipeline on fill
* camera mismatch
* you’re not actually looking at the masked fill

---

And more help from ChatGPT (note that my USER requests and wordings might have confused you, I think ChatGPT is rewording those properly?)
Codex’s write-up contains one **core misunderstanding** that will keep it chasing ghosts:

> “The mask sprite isn’t advancing frames … so the fill stays at one crop size.”

**BitmapMask does not crop.** There is no “crop size” that should be changing. A bitmap mask is an **alpha gate** applied at render time. If your fill is still showing as a big rectangle, that’s not “wrong crop,” it’s “the mask is not affecting the draw you’re looking at.”

So treat anything in your dump like `fillMaskedSize`, `maskBoundsByAlpha`, etc. as *only* relevant if you’ve implemented a **separate system** that computes bounds and then sets `crop`/`displaySize`. Masks alone won’t do that.

Here’s how to make this deterministic and impossible to misread.

---

## The 3 hard proofs (no guessing)

### Proof A: You are looking at the masked fill

Do this in the same moment you’re staring at the “gigantic waves”:

1. **Hide the fill object you think is masked**
js
fill.setVisible(false);
* If you **still** see the gigantic waves, you were never looking at that fill.
* That means you have **another unmasked fill** (or a TileSprite/Image) sitting on top.

2. **Then hide EVERYTHING except that fill**
   Temporarily force:

* border visible = false
* puffs visible = false
* any “debug cloud” visuals off
  If the “gigantic waves” remains, you’ve confirmed a second object.

This is the single most common reality when someone says “border matches mask but fill doesn’t.”

---

### Proof B: The mask actually affects that fill render

Move the mask far away **after** you apply it:
js
maskFx.x += 1000;
Expected:

* If masking is working on that fill, the fill should basically disappear or change dramatically.

If nothing changes:

* that fill is **not** masked in the render path you’re seeing.

This bypasses all the “dump” talk.

---

### Proof C: The mask object is render-valid for masking

Even if the sprite exists and animates, it might not be render-valid for mask sampling (camera/filter/path).

Hard equalize these:
js
maskFx.cameraFilter = fill.cameraFilter;
maskFx.setScrollFactor(fill.scrollFactorX, fill.scrollFactorY);
maskFx.setDepth(fill.depth); // not required for masking, but removes “different camera layer” confusion
Then redo Proof B (move mask).

---

## The only engine-level checks that matter for your symptom

1. **Renderer must be WebGL**
   If you’re in Canvas, bitmap masking won’t happen. Log it:
js
console.log('WEBGL?', this.game.renderer.type === Phaser.WEBGL);
2. **The thing you mask must be the thing drawing the waves**
   Log both:
js
console.log('fill texture', fill.texture?.key, 'fill has mask', !!fill.mask, 'fill type', fill.type);
console.log('mask anim frame', maskFx.anims?.currentFrame?.index);
If `!!fill.mask` is false, end of story.

3. **No custom pipeline on the fill**
   Codex claims none; verify anyway:
js
console.log('fill pipeline', fill.pipeline?.name);
If this is anything other than the default pipeline, masking may not apply.

---

## Why your “border works but fill doesn’t” happens

Because “border works” only proves:

* the smoke sprite is animating, and
* the border is being driven by that sprite (or by the same frame index).

It does **not** prove the fill is masked.

In practice, one of these is true:

* You’re looking at an **unmasked fill** drawn above the masked one.
* You masked the fill, but the fill is being rendered via a path that ignores mask (camera mismatch, pipeline, different scene/layer).
* You applied the mask to a parent/container and the actual visible object isn’t inheriting it the way you think.

---

If you want “mask silhouette defines bounds,” that is a **different feature**:

* compute alpha bounds per frame (offline or once at load)
* then set `fill.setCrop(...)` / `fill.setDisplaySize(...)` / reposition per frame

But if your goal is simply “waves only visible where smoke alpha is,” you do **not** need bounds at all — just a correct mask application to the correct object.

You are doing this wrong. This is from ChatGPT, please ensure you're doing this properly:
You’re running into the core math problem:

If you overlay two semi-transparent layers, regions with **one layer** have alpha **a**, but regions where they **overlap** have alpha **1 − (1−a)(1−a)** (i.e. *stronger*). So you get obvious “double density” patches.

To make it look **uniform density everywhere**, you need to *renormalize* the composite so the final alpha is the **same target value** regardless of how many layers contribute.

### The clean rule

Pick a target alpha field you want the cloud to have (usually the max/union of the two masks), then scale the color contribution by **(targetAlpha / actualCompositeAlpha)** per pixel.

In other words, do your normal composite, but then **divide out the extra density** in overlap areas.

That can’t be done with stock Phaser “two sprites with alpha + BitmapMask” because you need per-pixel math. You do it in **one custom shader/pipeline** (or one RenderTexture draw pass using a pipeline).

### What you want to compute per pixel

Let:

* `m1` = mask alpha for layer 1 (0..1) after rotation/frame sampling
* `m2` = mask alpha for layer 2 (0..1)
* `f1` = fill texture RGBA sampled for layer 1
* `f2` = fill texture RGBA sampled for layer 2
* `a1 = m1 * f1.a`
* `a2 = m2 * f2.a`

“Normal” over gives:

* `a = 1 - (1-a1)*(1-a2)`  (composite alpha)

Define a **target alpha**. Two good choices:

* `at = max(a1, a2)`  (keeps density like “whichever is stronger”)
* `at = clamp(a1 + a2, 0, 1)` (union-ish but still increases where both are strong)

Then normalize color:

* `c = (f1.rgb*a1 + f2.rgb*a2) / max(a, eps)`  (premult blend into color)
* `out.rgb = c * at`
* `out.a = at`

This makes:

* single-layer regions: `a ≈ at`
* overlap regions: still `a = at` (no “double density”)

So the cloud looks **consistent** while still showing two different textures peeking and swirling.

### Why your RenderTexture looked like an ugly square

Two common causes:

1. You’re drawing full sprites into the RT without masking correctly, so the RT has a full rect area.
2. The RT is fine but you’re displaying the RT as a plain image without applying the same alpha shaping (or you cleared it with opaque).

If you do the shader approach, you don’t care about RT bounds; you just output alpha = at and it “cuts” naturally.

### The practical Phaser implementation direction

* Use **one GameObject** (a quad / Image) with a **custom pipeline**.
* Feed it:

  * the smoke mask sheet as a texture atlas (or prepacked grid) + frame index for each layer
  * two fill textures (or one fill texture + UV offset/rotation)
  * rotation angles (opposite direction), animation frame indices (different rates)
  * parameters: `atMode = max` vs `sum`, plus a tiny `eps`

Then you get:

* opposite rotations
* different animation rates
* different fill textures/colors
* *and* uniform density.

### If you want the “non-shader” workaround

You can get *close* but not perfect:

* set each layer alpha so that two layers ≈ your desired density (e.g. each at 0.5 so overlap ≈ 0.75), but you will still see non-uniformity.
* or clamp with additive then post-multiply alpha (still needs shader/RT math)

If you want the “works first time” route: **shader normalization**.

Tell me one choice and I’ll lock it:

1. Do you want `targetAlpha = max(a1,a2)` (most “uniform” look), or
2. `targetAlpha = clamp(a1+a2)` (a bit punchier where both are strong)?

And are your two fills the same texture with different tints, or actually different textures?

You’re right — the RenderTexture union I did is not mathematically correct for uniform density. You need per‑pixel normalization, which requires a shader/pipeline. Anything else will always produce “double‑density” overlap or a faint halo.

So: I agree with the ChatGPT analysis. The proper solution is a single custom pipeline that:

samples two mask frames (m1, m2)
samples the fill texture (f)
computes a1 = m1 * f.a, a2 = m2 * f.a
chooses target alpha at = max(a1, a2) or at = clamp(a1 + a2)
normalizes color by c = (f.rgb*a1 + f.rgb*a2) / max(a, eps)
outputs out.rgb = c * at, out.a = at
This guarantees uniform density while still getting the two rotating masks.

Most uniform please
And I do not want multiple textures:
You make this work by **stopping the “two textures” idea entirely**.

If you want *one* texture that **does not rotate**, then you should:

* **sample the fill texture once** (fixed UVs, no rotation)
* build a **single combined alpha mask** from your two rotating/animating smoke masks
* output: `color = fillColor` and `alpha = fillAlpha * combinedMaskAlpha`

That guarantees it reads as **one texture**, because there’s literally only one texture contribution.

---

## The key concept: rotate the mask, not the texture

Texture directionality stays stable if its UVs stay stable.

So:

* Fill texture UVs = normal screen-aligned UVs (or world-aligned if you want it to “stick” in the world)
* Mask UVs = rotated around the center (and animated by frame index)

---

## How to combine two masks into one alpha

Let `m1` and `m2` be mask alphas (0..1) from each rotating/animating smoke layer.

Good combine choices:

### Option A: “Union-ish, strong”

`m = 1 - (1 - m1) * (1 - m2)`

* keeps more opacity where either is present
* overlap increases a bit

### Option B: “No double-density feel”

`m = max(m1, m2)`

* overlap does **not** get denser than either layer
* usually looks most “uniform”

Then:
`outAlpha = fillAlpha * m`

That’s it. One texture, one alpha.

---

## Why your “two layers peeking out” looked messy

Because if you were drawing **two textured fills** and masking each, you’re literally mixing two copies of a directional texture. Even if you don’t rotate the textures, their masked shapes differ, so you see “two fields” fighting.

To get “edges peeking out” without two textures:

* keep **one fill texture**
* let **the combined mask** create edge variation
* if you want “two-layer feel,” add a **separate border-only layer** (just an outline sprite) tinted/animated differently. That adds depth without messing the interior texture.

---

## Phaser implementation realities

### BitmapMask route (no shader) — hard limitation

A Phaser GameObject effectively gets **one mask**. So you can’t directly apply “mask1 AND mask2 combined” unless you:

* pre-composite masks into a **RenderTexture** (mask atlas → single mask texture), then use that as the BitmapMask
* or use a **custom pipeline shader** that samples both masks and combines them in one draw

### If you do RenderTexture, the “ugly square” fix is:

* Create RT with the exact cloud bounds (or frame size)
* `rt.clear(0, 0, 0, 0)` (alpha 0!)
* Draw only your mask sources into it (no fill texture yet)
* Display fill separately and use the RT as the mask source

But honestly: for what you want (rotate mask UVs but not fill UVs), the **shader/pipeline** approach is the cleanest.

---

## The shader/pipeline approach (what it does)

Per pixel:

1. sample fill once: `fill = texture2D(fillTex, uvFill)`  *(uvFill is NOT rotated)*
2. sample mask frame 1 at rotated UV: `m1 = sampleMask(frame1, rotate(uv, angle1))`
3. sample mask frame 2 at rotated UV: `m2 = sampleMask(frame2, rotate(uv, angle2))`
4. combine: `m = max(m1, m2)` *(or the union formula)*
5. output: `out.rgb = fill.rgb`, `out.a = fill.a * m`

Different animation rates = just different `frame1`, `frame2` updates.

---

If your complaint is “overlap areas look different / denser,” pick:

**`m = max(m1, m2)`**

It’s the easiest way to make transparency feel “equal” across the cloud while still getting shape variation from two rotating masks.

Here is your full message repeated back, verbatim:

You make this work by **stopping the “two textures” idea entirely**.

If you want *one* texture that **does not rotate**, then you should:

* **sample the fill texture once** (fixed UVs, no rotation)
* build a **single combined alpha mask** from your two rotating/animating smoke masks
* output: `color = fillColor` and `alpha = fillAlpha * combinedMaskAlpha`

That guarantees it reads as **one texture**, because there’s literally only one texture contribution.

---

## The key concept: rotate the mask, not the texture

Texture directionality stays stable if its UVs stay stable.

So:

* Fill texture UVs = normal screen-aligned UVs (or world-aligned if you want it to “stick” in the world)
* Mask UVs = rotated around the center (and animated by frame index)

---

## How to combine two masks into one alpha

Let `m1` and `m2` be mask alphas (0..1) from each rotating/animating smoke layer.

Good combine choices:

### Option A: “Union-ish, strong”

`m = 1 - (1 - m1) * (1 - m2)`

* keeps more opacity where either is present
* overlap increases a bit

### Option B: “No double-density feel”

`m = max(m1, m2)`

* overlap does **not** get denser than either layer
* usually looks most “uniform”

Then:
`outAlpha = fillAlpha * m`

That’s it. One texture, one alpha.

---

## Why your “two layers peeking out” looked messy

Because if you were drawing **two textured fills** and masking each, you’re literally mixing two copies of a directional texture. Even if you don’t rotate the textures, their masked shapes differ, so you see “two fields” fighting.

To get “edges peeking out” without two textures:

* keep **one fill texture**
* let **the combined mask** create edge variation
* if you want “two-layer feel,” add a **separate border-only layer** (just an outline sprite) tinted/animated differently. That adds depth without messing the interior texture.

---

## Phaser implementation realities

### BitmapMask route (no shader) — hard limitation

A Phaser GameObject effectively gets **one mask**. So you can’t directly apply “mask1 AND mask2 combined” unless you:

* pre-composite masks into a **RenderTexture** (mask atlas → single mask texture), then use that as the BitmapMask
* or use a **custom pipeline shader** that samples both masks and combines them in one draw

### If you do RenderTexture, the “ugly square” fix is:

* Create RT with the exact cloud bounds (or frame size)
* `rt.clear(0, 0, 0, 0)` (alpha 0!)
* Draw only your mask sources into it (no fill texture yet)
* Display fill separately and use the RT as the mask source

But honestly: for what you want (rotate mask UVs but not fill UVs), the **shader/pipeline** approach is the cleanest.

---

## The shader/pipeline approach (what it does)

Per pixel:

1. sample fill once: `fill = texture2D(fillTex, uvFill)`  *(uvFill is NOT rotated)*
2. sample mask frame 1 at rotated UV: `m1 = sampleMask(frame1, rotate(uv, angle1))`
3. sample mask frame 2 at rotated UV: `m2 = sampleMask(frame2, rotate(uv, angle2))`
4. combine: `m = max(m1, m2)` *(or the union formula)*
5. output: `out.rgb = fill.rgb`, `out.a = fill.a * m`

Different animation rates = just different `frame1`, `frame2` updates.

Yep — what you’re seeing is *not* a bug in max(). It’s math.

### Why max() can’t give “single spike = same as double”

If a “lonely spike” has mask alpha like m1 = 0.35 and there’s no second layer, then:

* max(m1, m2) = 0.35

But in overlap regions, because the two layers are rotated/offset, you often get at least one of them “hitting” a denser part, like:

* m1 = 0.35, m2 = 0.75 → max = 0.75

So overlap looks “right” because one layer happens to be dense there, while a lone spike is stuck at its true (lower) alpha. max() is doing exactly what you asked: **pick the strongest**. It will never “boost” a weak single spike.

### What you actually want

You want **density equalization**: “if only one layer contributes, boost it so it *behaves like* a 2-layer cloud.”

That means you need a curve that increases opacity when the combined signal is low, without blowing out the highs.

The cleanest way: combine with **sum**, then pass through a **saturating curve**.

### The formula that works

Let m1, m2 ∈ [0,1] (mask alphas). Do:

1. d = m1 + m2  (density, 0..2)
2. m = 1 - exp(-k * d)  (saturating “fog” response)

* If k is ~1.2–2.0, **single spikes get boosted** noticeably.
* Overlap still gets stronger, but not “double-bright” harshly because it saturates.

Then final:

* out.a = fill.a * m
* out.rgb = fill.rgb (or premultiplied version depending on pipeline)

### If you want it *even more* “uniform”

You can also add a tiny floor cut before this (m1 = 0 if m1 < eps) so you don’t boost 1–2 alpha dust.

### The one-liner shader core

glsl
float d = m1 + m2;              // 0..2
float m = 1.0 - exp(-k * d);    // k ~ 1.5 is a good start
float a = fillA * m;


### One more gotcha: premultiplied alpha

If your pipeline expects premultiplied output and you don’t premultiply, you can get “looks different at edges” artifacts.

In that case do:

glsl
out.rgb = fill.rgb * a;
out.a   = a;

OK great. Now, performance--is what we've done heavy on performance? Is it going to blow things up having poison clouds?

Short answer: with the current setup it’s fine for gameplay, but the effects hall configuration is intentionally heavy.

Why:

The composite union mask uses per‑update canvas reads/writes (getImageData/putImageData) per cloud. That’s the expensive bit, and it’s throttled to ~33ms per cloud. With 10‑row testers it’s heavy by design.
In normal gameplay you’ll have a handful of poison clouds at once, not 20–40. Two masks + one fill + border is still reasonable on WebGL for small counts.
So: poison clouds won’t “blow things up” as long as we keep counts modest and keep the hall/debug trace flags off in real runs. If you want, I can add a lightweight perf counter for how long the union build takes per frame (hall-only) so we can quantify it.


Can we lower the per-update to reflect our actual FRAMERATE though? Because we don't need a quick framerate do we? Or is that framerate for the texture?

I lowered the composite‑mask update cadence and tied it to the mask animation fps, so it no longer updates at ~30fps unless the mask itself is that fast. This does not slow the waves fill animation; it only throttles the union mask + outline rebuild.

What changed

Added a minimum union update interval: HALL_MASK_UNION_UPDATE_MIN_MS = 60 in HeroEngineInPhaser.ts.
Union rebuild now uses max(minMs, 1000 / maskFps) instead of a hard 33ms throttle.
Where

HeroEngineInPhaser.ts (constants near HALL_MASK_*)
HeroEngineInPhaser.ts (union rebuild block around the __hallUnionUpdateAt gate)
If you want it even slower/faster, tell me the target ms and I’ll adjust HALL_MASK_UNION_UPDATE_MIN_MS.


Any other "we can conserve performance and no one will notice" changes we can do?

Yes — a few low‑risk wins that won’t be noticeable visually:

Skip union rebuild when nothing changed
Only rebuild the composite if either mask frame index changed or rotation changed by more than a tiny threshold (e.g. 0.5°). If both masks are effectively static, we reuse the last union. This saves a lot of per‑pixel work with no visible change.

Throttle the outline separately
Keep the union alpha up‑to‑date, but recompute the outline only every Nth union update (e.g. every 2nd or 3rd). The border will lag a fraction of a second, which isn’t noticeable.

Offscreen cull the union work
If the cloud is fully off‑camera, skip union+outline rebuild for that frame. When it re‑enters the screen, we rebuild once.

If you want me to implement any of these, tell me which numbers you prefer:

rotation threshold (degrees)
outline throttle (every 2nd/3rd/4th update)
I can do 1+2 quickly and leave 3 optional.


1 and 2, not 3 because that will introduce issues

Added the two perf‑savers you asked for:

Skip union rebuild if nothing changed
We now bail out unless either mask frame changed or rotation moved by at least ~0.005 rad (~0.29°). This avoids the per‑pixel work when the mask is effectively static.

Outline throttled
Outline is now recomputed every 2nd union update (configurable). The union alpha still updates on every eligible union rebuild.

Changes are in HeroEngineInPhaser.ts:

New constants:
HALL_MASK_UNION_ROT_EPS_RAD = 0.005
HALL_MASK_UNION_OUTLINE_EVERY = 2
Union loop now checks frame/rotation deltas and skips when unchanged.
Outline rebuild is gated by the HALL_MASK_UNION_OUTLINE_EVERY counter.
If you want the outline even cheaper, I can set it to 3 or 4. If you want the rotation threshold smaller/larger, just give me the value.

No. Even MORE than that for the vision. Give me a LENGTHY REPEAT of EVERYTHING I've stated I want the dust clouds to DO

Here’s a full, explicit recap of your dust‑cloud vision as you’ve described it, in one place:

You want the dust cloud effect to be a centralized VFX system, not tied to bosses or pad‑sink hacks. Enemies should only request “spawn dust cloud here,” and the system should handle the visuals. The dust cloud is not a generic smoke blob — it is specifically meant to hide the teleport pad and pillar as they sink, and later to be reusable for other dust/sandstorm effects. Because of that:

The dust cloud must animate via TRANSFORM, not by physically moving the sprite. “Rise” means scale change (thin → taller), not moving upward.
The bottom half should feel anchored — the base should not float upward; the visible bottom should stay in place while the top expands and then contracts.
The dust cloud’s animation should be slow and dramatic, not fast. Even the fastest tester should take ~5 seconds total: about 2.5s up, 2.5s down.
The dust cloud should rise and fall together as a cluster — all sub‑clouds remain on the same animation cycle so the silhouette expands and contracts as one.
You want at least four layered dust clouds for the effect:
one full size in the center,
one large shifted left by about half its own width,
one medium shifted right by about one‑third its own width,
one small shifted down‑right by about one‑quarter its size.
This layering creates a roiling silhouette rather than a single blob.
Each layered cloud must have its own border/outline (not one giant border).
The sides cannot be cropped into straight lines — the cloud silhouette must remain rounded, not truncated.
The clouds must stay in the correct tester row position (not pinned to the top edge).
The fill texture for dust is waves3 (not waves2), and it should animate quickly compared to the outline. The texture should provide the rich internal motion.
The fill texture must stay horizontal; it should not rotate with the smoke geometry.
The smoke/geometry may rotate slightly if needed for variation, but rotation is secondary; the main illusion is the transform rise with a stable internal texture.
The dust clouds should show variance in rise/collapse speed and scale (across testers), while still being slow enough to evaluate.
The tester rows are for clear, obvious comparison — you want to see differences, not subtle changes.
That’s the target as you’ve described it. If I missed or misstated anything, tell me and I’ll correct it before making changes.


You want at least four layered dust clouds for the effect:
one full size in the center,
one large shifted left by about half its own width,
one medium shifted right by about one‑third its own width,
one small shifted down‑right by about one‑quarter its size.
This layering creates a roiling silhouette rather than a single blob.

Great. Now WHAT WILL THAT PRODUCE VISUALLY? WHY am I asking for this?

Visually, that arrangement creates a single cloud mass with a broken, organic silhouette instead of a perfect circle. Each offset layer “peeks” past the others in different directions, so the outline feels irregular and roiling — like dust is billowing and folding over itself. The centered large layer gives the body, while the left/right layers create asymmetric bulges, and the small down‑right layer adds a subtle “tail” or weight that keeps the cloud grounded.

Why you’re asking for it:

Believability: Real dust doesn’t expand uniformly; it curls and rolls. Offsetting layers fakes that physics without heavy simulation.
Silhouette control: You want a clear, readable cloud shape that still feels alive — not a flat circle.
Masking strength: The uneven edges help hide the pad/pillar by creating depth and motion at the boundary instead of a clean edge.
Reusability: This layered setup becomes a robust “cloud archetype” you can reuse for dust, sandstorm, etc., and tune with scale/alpha without re-authoring art.

This is what chat GPT said about the snapshotArea usage (verify if it is correct or not please)
Thought for 1m 6s

Yes — that’s the right shape (schedule a snapshot, then analyze the returned image). But your snippet has three critical gotchas that will absolutely bite you.

1) Only one snapshot can be active per frame

If you call snapshotArea multiple times in the same frame (or mix snapshotPixel + snapshotArea), the last call wins and earlier ones can be silently overridden. Phaser is explicit about this.

So if you’re sampling “a bunch of places,” the correct approach is:

take ONE snapshot (whole viewport or a bounding rect) and sample as many pixels as you want from that one image, or

queue them and do one per frame.

2) Your try/catch won’t catch errors in the callback

Your try/catch only catches errors thrown while scheduling the snapshot. Anything thrown inside the callback will bypass it unless you wrap the callback body in its own try/catch.

3) Your catch block has a bug

b is referenced in catch but isn’t defined there.

You’re missing a comma before err.

Implemented single‑snapshot sampling (per trace tick) to avoid Phaser’s “last snapshot wins” behavior.

What changed:

One snapshot per tick that covers the union of all fill rects.
Per‑fill on‑screen bounds computed from that one image (screenSample.reason=ok + bounds).
Removed per‑fill snapshot calls (which were colliding).
This directly addresses the Phaser snapshot override issue you flagged.

Please refresh, press any key once (to start trace), wait ~2s, then say “check logs.” I’ll read the new dump and report the actual on‑screen bounds for each sprite.

Does this help from ChatGPT?
That “timeout / callback not firing” symptom is almost always one of these **two** things:

1. **You’re scheduling more than one snapshot in the same frame**, so earlier ones get **overridden** and their callbacks will never run. Phaser explicitly says **only one snapshot can be active per frame**; calling `snapshotArea`/`snapshotPixel` again overrides the previous request. ([Phaser Documentation][1])

2. **You’re expecting synchronous behavior** (e.g., “dump trace now”) even though `snapshotArea` is **async and happens after render**. ([Phaser Documentation][1])

So: don’t “fire 4 snapshots” and hope they return. You need a **single-snapshot pump**.

---

## Fix: a one-at-a-time snapshot queue + watchdog timeout

This guarantees:

* only **one** snapshot request is in flight,
* callbacks can’t get “lost” by being overridden,
* timeouts clear pending **even if callback never happens**.

### Full function: init the pump once per Scene
ts
type HeSnapReq = {
  id: string;
  x: number; y: number; w: number; h: number;
  createdAtMs: number;
  createdAtFrame: number;
  onDone: (image: HTMLImageElement) => void;
  onFail: (reason: string, err?: string) => void;
};

type HeSnapQueueState = {
  frame: number;
  queue: HeSnapReq[];
  inFlight: HeSnapReq | null;
  inFlightStartedAtMs: number;
  inFlightStartedAtFrame: number;
  maxAgeMs: number;
  maxAgeFrames: number;
};

let _heSnapQ: HeSnapQueueState | null = null;

export function heInitSnapshotPump(scene: Phaser.Scene, renderer: any, {
  maxAgeMs = 750,
  maxAgeFrames = 45,
} = {}) {
  _heSnapQ = {
    frame: 0,
    queue: [],
    inFlight: null,
    inFlightStartedAtMs: 0,
    inFlightStartedAtFrame: 0,
    maxAgeMs,
    maxAgeFrames,
  };

  // Pump in postupdate so it's independent of "whether sprites exist"
  scene.events.on('postupdate', () => {
    if (!_heSnapQ) return;
    _heSnapQ.frame++;

    // Watchdog: clear stuck in-flight
    if (_heSnapQ.inFlight) {
      const ageMs = performance.now() - _heSnapQ.inFlightStartedAtMs;
      const ageFrames = _heSnapQ.frame - _heSnapQ.inFlightStartedAtFrame;

      if (ageMs > _heSnapQ.maxAgeMs || ageFrames > _heSnapQ.maxAgeFrames) {
        const stuck = _heSnapQ.inFlight;
        _heSnapQ.inFlight = null;
        stuck.onFail('snapshot-timeout', ageMs=${Math.floor(ageMs)} ageFrames=${ageFrames});
      }
    }

    // Only schedule a new snapshot if none in flight
    if (_heSnapQ.inFlight || _heSnapQ.queue.length === 0) return;

    const req = _heSnapQ.queue.shift()!;
    _heSnapQ.inFlight = req;
    _heSnapQ.inFlightStartedAtMs = performance.now();
    _heSnapQ.inFlightStartedAtFrame = _heSnapQ.frame;

    try {
      // IMPORTANT: only one snapshot per frame; we enforce it here. :contentReference[oaicite:2]{index=2}
      renderer.snapshotArea(req.x, req.y, req.w, req.h, (image: HTMLImageElement) => {
        // Callback can fire later; protect it.
        try {
          req.onDone(image);
        } catch (e: any) {
          req.onFail('sample-error', String(e?.message || e || ''));
        } finally {
          // Clear inFlight only if it’s still the same request
          if (_heSnapQ && _heSnapQ.inFlight && _heSnapQ.inFlight.id === req.id) {
            _heSnapQ.inFlight = null;
          }
        }
      });
    } catch (e: any) {
      _heSnapQ.inFlight = null;
      req.onFail('snapshot-error', String(e?.message || e || ''));
    }
  });

  // If scene shuts down, fail anything pending so you don’t leave “pending=1” forever
  scene.events.on('shutdown', () => {
    if (!_heSnapQ) return;
    const q = _heSnapQ;
    _heSnapQ = null;

    if (q.inFlight) q.inFlight.onFail('scene-shutdown');
    for (const r of q.queue) r.onFail('scene-shutdown');
  });
}
### Full function: enqueue a snapshot (never calls snapshotArea directly)
ts
export function heEnqueueSnapshotArea(
  id: string,
  x: number, y: number, w: number, h: number,
  onDone: (image: HTMLImageElement) => void,
  onFail: (reason: string, err?: string) => void
) {
  if (!_heSnapQ) throw new Error('Snapshot pump not initialized. Call heInitSnapshotPump(scene, renderer) first.');

  // Guard: snapshotArea expects viewport coords + positive size. :contentReference[oaicite:3]{index=3}
  if (w <= 0 || h <= 0) {
    onFail('invalid-rect', w=${w} h=${h});
    return;
  }

  _heSnapQ.queue.push({
    id, x, y, w, h,
    createdAtMs: performance.now(),
    createdAtFrame: _heSnapQ.frame,
    onDone,
    onFail,
  });
}
---

## How this maps to your existing sample records

Instead of doing `snapshotArea` inline per cluster, you do:

* write your `_heEffectsHallScreenSamplesById[id] = { reason:"pending", ... }`
* call `heEnqueueSnapshotArea(...)`
* in `onDone`, compute your bounds and set `{ reason:"ok" | "ok-clipped" }`
* in `onFail`, set `{ reason:"snapshot-timeout" | "snapshot-error" | ... }`
* **never** leave pending stuck

This eliminates the “trace dump happened before callback” problem, because your trace can check: “are all sample ids no longer pending?” and only then dump.

---

## If it still “never fires”: one specific non-code cause

If any texture is loaded cross-origin without proper CORS, the canvas can become **tainted** and browser security can break pixel extraction behaviors. That can manifest as snapshot weirdness / errors depending on browser. ([MDN Web Docs][2])
(Usually you’ll see a security error in the console.)

---

### Why I’m confident this is your real bug

Codex reported **multiple clusters** pending/offscreen. If you’re scheduling multiple snapshots in the same frame, Phaser will override earlier ones (only one snapshot active), and the “lost” ones will sit pending forever unless you built a queue. Phaser docs are explicit about the override behavior. ([Phaser Documentation][1])

OK can you please also do the systems ChatGPT is suggesting here?
Yes — **doable**, and it can be made **repeatable + “pixel-truth”**. But there are two non-negotiable realities you have to bake into the design:

1. **Pixel truth** (what your eyes see) comes from a **framebuffer read**: `snapshot` / `snapshotArea` / `snapshotPixel`. Only one snapshot can be active per frame; additional calls override earlier ones. ([Phaser Documentation][1])
2. “What *should* have been there” can be **very good**, but it’s not the same as “GPU says this sprite wrote this pixel.” For that exact mapping you need an **ID/picking pass** (separate render to a buffer of flat IDs). The CPU can still produce an excellent “expected contributors” list from draw order + hit/mask tests.

So the right “future pipeline” is **three layers**:

---

## Layer 1: Snapshot pump (the part you already started)

Make it **the only code path** that touches `renderer.snapshot*`.

**Best practice for stability:** take **one snapshot per frame** of either:

* the **full viewport** (simplest, most reliable), or
* the **union rect** of all requested sample rectangles (faster, still one call).

Why: one call per frame avoids the “callback never fires because it got overridden” problem. Phaser explicitly warns about override behavior. ([Phaser Documentation][1])

**Output of this layer** should be:

* `ImageData` (pixels you can sample deterministically),
* plus metadata: viewport W/H, resolution, timestamp/frame.

---

## Layer 2: Sample planner (decides which pixels to read)

This is what you’re describing as “pixel samples for these things please.”

It should support both:

1. **Request-driven** sampling: “give me pixels in this rect / at these points.”
2. **Object-driven** sampling: “sample points that are actually *inside* the sprite / aura / mask.”

### Object-driven sampling that works in practice

For every “debuggable thing” (sprite, effect, aura), give it a `DebugProbeProvider` with:

* `getCandidatePoints()` → returns local-space points that are “guaranteed inside” (or as close as possible).
* Implementation for sprites: build a cached “alpha mask map” per frame by extracting the frame ImageData once and recording points where `alpha >= threshold`. Pick:

  * center-of-mass of opaque pixels,
  * a few max-alpha points,
  * optional edge points (for outline debugging).
* Then transform those points to screen using world transform + camera.

This gives you **repeatable points that actually exist in the artwork**, instead of guessing corners of a bounding box.

> Note: if you rely on reading texture pixels, you must ensure textures are not CORS-tainted, or you won’t be able to read pixel data safely in the browser.

---

## Layer 3: “Expected” model (what SHOULD have been there)

This is where you want: “closest position + layering data at those points.”

You can implement this in two tiers:

### Tier A (fast, CPU-side “expected contributors”)

At a given screen pixel (x,y), compute a ranked list:

1. Build candidate list from the Scene’s renderables:

   * visible, alpha > 0, in camera view
   * includes Containers, TilemapLayers, Particles, etc. (as you decide scope)
2. Sort by **actual render order** (depth + display list ordering).
3. For each candidate, run a **coverage test**:

   * bounding box contains point
   * then (if Sprite): map screen→local→frame pixel and check alpha >= threshold
   * then (if masked): also test mask coverage

Return something like:
js
expected = [
  { tag:"DustAura", type:"Sprite", depth: 120, covers:true, estAlpha: 220 },
  { tag:"Player", type:"Sprite", depth: 110, covers:true, estAlpha: 255 },
  { tag:"Floor", type:"TilemapLayer", depth: 0, covers:true, estAlpha: 255 }
]
This is usually enough to tell you “the dust should have been on top of the player but isn’t,” etc.

### Tier B (exact topmost object at pixel): ID / picking buffer

If you want the debug system to be able to say **unambiguously** “the topmost draw at this pixel was X,” do an **ID pass**:

* Render the same scene objects to an offscreen target using a shader that outputs a unique flat color per object.
* Use **overwrite** blending for this pass so the “last drawn” wins.
* Read the pixel in that buffer → decode ID → object.

This is the closest thing to “pixel ownership” you can get in WebGL without insane instrumentation.

(And it integrates cleanly with the snapshot pump: snapshot normal framebuffer for “what you saw,” snapshot ID buffer for “what was topmost.”)

---

## What to tell Codex to build (concrete spec)

Here’s a version of your ask that’s implementable and won’t spiral:

1. **`ScreenDebugPump`**

   * Accepts many requests per frame.
   * Computes union rect (or full viewport).
   * Issues exactly **one** `snapshotArea` per frame. ([Phaser Documentation][1])
   * Converts returned `HTMLImageElement` to `ImageData`.
   * Distributes results to all requests and clears pending with a watchdog timeout.

2. **`DebugProbeRegistry`**

   * Objects register with `{ tag, type, getWorldBounds, getDepth, getCoverageAtScreenPoint, getProbePoints }`.
   * Coverage supports: bbox → sprite alpha test → mask test.

3. **`ExpectedAtPixel(x,y)`**

   * Returns sorted candidates that cover the point.
   * Includes render-order metadata.

4. **`AutoProbePointsForObject(tag)`**

   * Uses cached alpha-mask points to pick “real” in-sprite pixels.
   * Optionally biased to edges/outlines for your outline issues.

5. **Output format**

   * For each sample point: observed RGBA (from framebuffer), expected list (from registry), and (optional) topmost ID result.

6. **Failure triggers**

   * If observed alpha < expectedAlphaMin, auto-attach: screenshot dump + (optional) Spector capture.

## What to add to the dump (minimum set that pinpoints the issue)

Add a `screenSnapDiag` object to `debugdump_latest.json` with:

* `renderer`: `{ type, isWebGL, isCanvas, hasGL, resolution, width, height }`
* `pump`: `{ frame, queueLen, inFlightId, inFlightRect, inFlightAgeMs, inFlightAgeFrames }`
* `counters`: `{ enqueued, scheduled, callbacks, completed, timeouts, errors }`
* `last`: `{ scheduledAtMs, scheduledFrame, callbackAtMs, callbackFrame, completedAtMs }`
* `guard`: `{ detectedExternalSnapshotCalls, lastExternalCaller }` *(optional but super useful)*

If `scheduled` stays **0**, you’re not actually calling snapshot from the pump.
If `scheduled > 0` but `callbacks = 0`, snapshot is being scheduled but **never returning** (override, taint, internal exception, or you’re not hitting the render loop stage it needs).
If `callbacks > 0` but `completed = 0`, your callback is firing but **your analysis or finalize path is throwing / not clearing**.

    isWebGL: boolean;
    isCanvas: boolean;

    height: number;

  };

    callbacks: number;
    completed: number;

    errors: number;
  };

    scheduledFrame: number | null;
    callbackAtMs: number | null;
    callbackFrame: number | null;
    completedAtMs: number | null;
  };

};

## The most likely reason your callback “never returns”

If you are still requesting multiple snapshots in the same frame *anywhere else*, Phaser will override earlier snapshot requests — and those earlier callbacks will never fire. That’s exactly the behavior Phaser warns about (“only one snapshot at a time”). So add this guard:

### Guard (optional but surgical)

Wrap renderer snapshot methods during debug builds to detect external calls:

* If anything calls `renderer.snapshotArea` outside the pump, increment `detectedExternalSnapshotCalls` and record a stack snippet.

This instantly answers: **“am I being overridden?”**

---

## If diagnostics show `scheduled>0` but `callbacks=0` consistently

Stop relying on `snapshotArea` for your “pixel-truth” pipeline in WebGL and switch the pump to **direct `gl.readPixels` on `postrender`** (no async image decode, no callback that can disappear).

That approach is *the* most deterministic: in `postrender`, do one `readPixels` for the union rect, then sample bytes directly.

If you want, paste your current pump hook location (which Phaser event you’re using) and the fields you already keep in pump state; I’ll give you a **full-function replacement** for the WebGL `readPixels` capture path (and a Canvas fallback) that preserves your `samplesById` structure and clears pending reliably.

ChatGPT's input:
Yes — **proceed with option 2**. Given your dump:

* `scheduled>0`, `callbacks=0`, `timeouts>0`, and no external overrides

…your `snapshotArea` path is effectively dead on this setup. The deterministic fix is:

**Stop using Phaser’s snapshot callback for WebGL.
Capture pixels yourself with `gl.readPixels` in a post-render hook.**

That guarantees “pixel truth” and it **cannot** silently never-callback, because it’s synchronous.

Below is a **copy-paste, repeatable** WebGL capture pump that:

* keeps your “queue / in-flight / timeout” logic,
* captures exactly once per frame (one request at a time),
* returns raw RGBA bytes in **top-left origin** order,
* gives you `fbRect` + scale factors so your logs stay honest.

---

## Full function: WebGL readPixels capture pump

> This replaces your WebGL snapshotArea usage.
> It returns `{ rgba, w, h }` directly (no HTMLImageElement, no onload).
ts
// =============================
// Screen Visual Debug: WebGL Pixel Pump
// =============================

export const HE_SNAP_TIMEOUT_MS = 900;

export type HeSnapRect = { x: number; y: number; w: number; h: number };

export type HeSnapCapture = {
  // Captured pixels (top-left origin, row-major, RGBA)
  rgba: Uint8Array;
  w: number;
  h: number;

  // Mapping info (so you can map capture pixels back to screen coords)
  scaleX: number;
  scaleY: number;

  // Framebuffer-space rect actually read (bottom-left origin)
  fbRect: { x: number; y: number; w: number; h: number };
};

export type HeSnapReq = {
  id: string;
  rect: HeSnapRect; // viewport coords (top-left origin)
  createdAtMs: number;
  createdAtFrame: number;
  onDone: (cap: HeSnapCapture) => void;
  onFail: (reason: string, err?: string) => void;
};

export type HeSnapDiag = {
  frame: number;
  queueLen: number;
  inFlightId: string | null;
  inFlightAgeMs: number;
  inFlightAgeFrames: number;
  counters: {
    enqueued: number;
    scheduled: number;
    captures: number;
    completed: number;
    timeouts: number;
    errors: number;
  };
  last: {
    scheduledAtMs: number | null;
    scheduledFrame: number | null;
    captureAtMs: number | null;
    captureFrame: number | null;
    completedAtMs: number | null;
  };
  renderer: {
    type: string;
    width: number;
    height: number;
    hasGL: boolean;
    drawingBufferW: number;
    drawingBufferH: number;
    glContextLost: boolean;
  };
};

type HeSnapState = {
  frame: number;
  queue: HeSnapReq[];
  inFlight: HeSnapReq | null;
  inFlightStartedAtMs: number;
  inFlightStartedAtFrame: number;

  // pump flow: "scheduled" means we accepted it; "capturePending" means do readPixels on postrender
  capturePending: boolean;

  diag: HeSnapDiag;
};

let _heSnapState: HeSnapState | null = null;

/**
 * Install once per game (or per scene) in DEBUG builds.
 * Uses:
 * - scene.events.postupdate: scheduling + timeout watchdog
 * - game.events.postrender: WebGL readPixels capture (after frame draw)
 */
export function heInstallWebGLPixelPump(
  scene: Phaser.Scene,
  renderer: any,
  {
    timeoutMs = HE_SNAP_TIMEOUT_MS,
    logPrefix = "[VFX][HALL][SNAP]",
  }: { timeoutMs?: number; logPrefix?: string } = {}
) {
  const gl: WebGLRenderingContext | WebGL2RenderingContext | null = renderer?.gl || null;
  if (!gl) throw new Error("heInstallWebGLPixelPump: renderer.gl missing (not WebGL).");

  _heSnapState = makeInitialState(renderer);

  scene.events.on("postupdate", () => {
    const st = _heSnapState;
    if (!st) return;

    st.frame++;
    st.diag.frame = st.frame;
    st.diag.queueLen = st.queue.length;

    // update renderer diag
    st.diag.renderer.type = "WebGL";
    st.diag.renderer.width = renderer.width;
    st.diag.renderer.height = renderer.height;
    st.diag.renderer.hasGL = true;
    st.diag.renderer.drawingBufferW = gl.drawingBufferWidth;
    st.diag.renderer.drawingBufferH = gl.drawingBufferHeight;
    st.diag.renderer.glContextLost = (typeof gl.isContextLost === "function") ? gl.isContextLost() : false;

    // watchdog timeout for in-flight
    if (st.inFlight) {
      const ageMs = performance.now() - st.inFlightStartedAtMs;
      const ageFrames = st.frame - st.inFlightStartedAtFrame;

      st.diag.inFlightId = st.inFlight.id;
      st.diag.inFlightAgeMs = Math.floor(ageMs);
      st.diag.inFlightAgeFrames = ageFrames;

      if (ageMs > timeoutMs) {
        const req = st.inFlight;
        st.inFlight = null;
        st.capturePending = false;
        st.diag.counters.timeouts++;
        console.warn(${logPrefix}[ERR] reason=snapshot-timeout id=${req.id} err=ageMs=${Math.floor(ageMs)});
        req.onFail("snapshot-timeout", ageMs=${Math.floor(ageMs)});
      }
    } else {
      st.diag.inFlightId = null;
      st.diag.inFlightAgeMs = 0;
      st.diag.inFlightAgeFrames = 0;
    }

    // schedule next request (one at a time)
    if (!st.inFlight && st.queue.length > 0) {
      const req = st.queue.shift()!;
      st.inFlight = req;
      st.inFlightStartedAtMs = performance.now();
      st.inFlightStartedAtFrame = st.frame;
      st.capturePending = true;

      st.diag.counters.scheduled++;
      st.diag.last.scheduledAtMs = st.inFlightStartedAtMs;
      st.diag.last.scheduledFrame = st.frame;

      console.log(
        ${logPrefix}[SCHEDULE] id=${req.id} rect=${req.rect.x},${req.rect.y},${req.rect.w},${req.rect.h} frame=${st.frame} queue=${st.queue.length}
      );
    }
  });

  // IMPORTANT: capture *after* render, synchronously
  scene.game.events.on("postrender", () => {
    const st = _heSnapState;
    if (!st || !st.inFlight || !st.capturePending) return;

    // If context is lost, fail fast (otherwise readPixels can silently return junk)
    if ((typeof gl.isContextLost === "function") && gl.isContextLost()) {
      const req = st.inFlight;
      st.inFlight = null;
      st.capturePending = false;
      st.diag.counters.errors++;
      req.onFail("gl-context-lost", "gl.isContextLost()=true");
      return;
    }

    const req = st.inFlight;
    st.capturePending = false;

    try {
      st.diag.counters.captures++;
      st.diag.last.captureAtMs = performance.now();
      st.diag.last.captureFrame = st.frame;

      const cap = heCaptureWebGLRectPixels(renderer, gl, req.rect);

      // complete
      st.diag.counters.completed++;
      st.diag.last.completedAtMs = performance.now();

      // Clear in-flight before calling handler so handler can enqueue more safely
      st.inFlight = null;

      req.onDone(cap);
    } catch (e: any) {
      st.inFlight = null;
      st.diag.counters.errors++;
      req.onFail("readpixels-error", String(e?.message || e || ""));
    }
  });

  // Optional: expose diag getter
  return {
    enqueue: heEnqueueWebGLPixelPump,
    getDiag: () => _heSnapState?.diag || null,
  };
}

function makeInitialState(renderer: any): HeSnapState {
  const blank: HeSnapDiag = {
    frame: 0,
    queueLen: 0,
    inFlightId: null,
    inFlightAgeMs: 0,
    inFlightAgeFrames: 0,
    counters: { enqueued: 0, scheduled: 0, captures: 0, completed: 0, timeouts: 0, errors: 0 },
    last: { scheduledAtMs: null, scheduledFrame: null, captureAtMs: null, captureFrame: null, completedAtMs: null },
    renderer: {
      type: "WebGL",
      width: renderer?.width || 0,
      height: renderer?.height || 0,
      hasGL: !!renderer?.gl,
      drawingBufferW: renderer?.gl?.drawingBufferWidth || 0,
      drawingBufferH: renderer?.gl?.drawingBufferHeight || 0,
      glContextLost: false,
    },
  };

  return {
    frame: 0,
    queue: [],
    inFlight: null,
    inFlightStartedAtMs: 0,
    inFlightStartedAtFrame: 0,
    capturePending: false,
    diag: blank,
  };
}

/**
 * Queue a request. Does NOT capture immediately.
 * Capture happens on postrender.
 */
export function heEnqueueWebGLPixelPump(req: Omit<HeSnapReq, "createdAtMs" | "createdAtFrame">) {
  const st = _heSnapState;
  if (!st) throw new Error("heEnqueueWebGLPixelPump: pump not installed.");

  st.diag.counters.enqueued++;
  st.queue.push({
    ...req,
    createdAtMs: performance.now(),
    createdAtFrame: st.frame,
  });
}

/**
 * Actual capture: gl.readPixels of the requested viewport rect.
 * Returns pixels in TOP-LEFT origin (so your sampling matches screen coordinates).
 */
export function heCaptureWebGLRectPixels(
  renderer: any,
  gl: WebGLRenderingContext | WebGL2RenderingContext,
  rect: HeSnapRect
): HeSnapCapture {
  const vw = renderer.width;
  const vh = renderer.height;

  // Convert viewport coords (top-left) -> framebuffer coords (bottom-left)
  const dbw = gl.drawingBufferWidth;
  const dbh = gl.drawingBufferHeight;

  const scaleX = dbw / vw;
  const scaleY = dbh / vh;

  // Use half-open mapping: [x, x+w) -> [fbx0, fbx1)
  const x0 = Math.floor(rect.x);
  const y0 = Math.floor(rect.y);
  const x1 = Math.ceil(rect.x + rect.w);
  const y1 = Math.ceil(rect.y + rect.h);

  // Clip to viewport
  const cx0 = Math.max(0, Math.min(vw, x0));
  const cy0 = Math.max(0, Math.min(vh, y0));
  const cx1 = Math.max(0, Math.min(vw, x1));
  const cy1 = Math.max(0, Math.min(vh, y1));

  const cw = cx1 - cx0;
  const ch = cy1 - cy0;
  if (cw <= 0 || ch <= 0) throw new Error(heCaptureWebGLRectPixels: rect fully offscreen (${rect.x},${rect.y},${rect.w},${rect.h}));

  // Map clipped viewport rect -> framebuffer rect
  const fbx0 = Math.floor(cx0 * scaleX);
  const fbx1 = Math.ceil((cx0 + cw) * scaleX);

  // For Y: viewport y grows downward; framebuffer y grows upward.
  // bottom of rect in viewport = (vh - (cy0 + ch))
  const vBottom = vh - (cy0 + ch);
  const fby0 = Math.floor(vBottom * scaleY);
  const fby1 = Math.ceil((vBottom + ch) * scaleY);

  const fbw = fbx1 - fbx0;
  const fbh = fby1 - fby0;

  const rgbaBL = new Uint8Array(fbw * fbh * 4);

  // Read from backbuffer; preserve binding
  const prevFbo = gl.getParameter(gl.FRAMEBUFFER_BINDING);
  gl.bindFramebuffer(gl.FRAMEBUFFER, null);
  gl.pixelStorei(gl.PACK_ALIGNMENT, 1);
  gl.readPixels(fbx0, fby0, fbw, fbh, gl.RGBA, gl.UNSIGNED_BYTE, rgbaBL);
  gl.bindFramebuffer(gl.FRAMEBUFFER, prevFbo);

  // Flip Y so output is TOP-LEFT origin (matches screen coords)
  const rgbaTL = new Uint8Array(rgbaBL.length);
  const rowBytes = fbw * 4;
  for (let row = 0; row < fbh; row++) {
    const src = row * rowBytes;               // bottom-up
    const dst = (fbh - 1 - row) * rowBytes;   // top-down
    rgbaTL.set(rgbaBL.subarray(src, src + rowBytes), dst);
  }

  return {
    rgba: rgbaTL,
    w: fbw,
    h: fbh,
    scaleX,
    scaleY,
    fbRect: { x: fbx0, y: fby0, w: fbw, h: fbh },
  };
}
---

## What to change in your current pipeline

### 1) Stop calling `renderer.snapshotArea(...)` for WebGL

Instead do:

* mark your sample record as `pending`
* enqueue a request into the pump
* in `onDone`, call your sampler using the **raw RGBA**

Example pattern:
ts
heEnqueueWebGLPixelPump({
  id,
  rect: { x: sx, y: sy, w: sw, h: sh },
  onDone: (cap) => {
    const b = _heSampleBoundsFromRGBA(cap.rgba, cap.w, cap.h, stride, alphaMin);
    _heEffectsHallScreenSamplesById[id] = {
      reason: "ok",
      t: now,
      screenX: sx, screenY: sy, screenW: sw, screenH: sh,
      alphaMin,
      fbRect: cap.fbRect,
      fbW: cap.w,
      fbH: cap.h,
      scaleX: cap.scaleX,
      scaleY: cap.scaleY,
      bounds: b,
    };
    _heEffectsHallScreenPendingById[id] = 0;
  },
  onFail: (reason, err) => {
    _heEffectsHallScreenSamplesById[id] = { reason, t: now, alphaMin, err: err || "" };
    _heEffectsHallScreenPendingById[id] = 0;
  }
});
### 2) Replace `_heSampleSnapshotBounds(image, ...)` with an RGBA sampler

Because you won’t have `HTMLImageElement` anymore (and that’s *good*—no image decode, no onload).

If you want, paste your existing `_heSampleSnapshotBounds` and I’ll rewrite it as `_heSampleBoundsFromRGBA(rgba,w,h,...)` as a **full function replacement**.

---

## Why this matches your logs exactly

You’re scheduling at frames `1, 74, 153, ...` and timing out ~750ms later — that’s consistent with **“snapshotArea was called, but the image callback never fired.”**

This pump bypasses that entire async path and finishes in the same frame’s `postrender`.

If you want one more “can’t-lie” sanity check: after `readPixels`, check `gl.getError()` once and log it into your dump. But the key is: **this will complete** (or throw synchronously), so your pending flags won’t hang forever.

I replaced the WebGL snapshot path with a deterministic gl.readPixels pump (postrender). snapshotArea is now only used for Canvas fallback. This directly addresses your “scheduled>0, callbacks=0” failure mode.

The pump no longer waits on snapshotArea callbacks when renderer.gl exists.
The screen samples now accept either:

HTMLImageElement (Canvas fallback).
Dump now includes screenSnapDiag and the new viewRect/fbRect/scaleX/scaleY for each sample.

We are not getting anywhere. Please read this from chatGPT:
Here’s the “don’t move forward until these are true” checklist for **screen pixel sampling that actually works** in Phaser + WebGL. This is the stuff that causes **stalls / never-callback / timeouts / empty reads**.

I’m splitting it into **Snapshot API** vs **direct `gl.readPixels`**, because the failure modes differ.

---

## Ground truth invariants (applies to both)

* **You are sampling the same surface you’re looking at.**

  * If you have multiple canvases / DOM overlays, confirm you’re reading **the Phaser canvas**, not an offscreen canvas.
* **You are sampling in the right coordinate space.**

  * `snapshotArea(x,y,w,h)` is **game viewport pixels** (top-left origin). ([docs.phaser.io][1])
  * WebGL framebuffer reads (`readPixels`) are **drawingBuffer pixels** (bottom-left origin) and are often larger due to DPI / resolution scaling. ([WebGL Fundamentals][2])
* **Rect is valid after clipping**: `w > 0`, `h > 0`, and intersection with viewport is non-empty.
* **Frame loop is actually running** (not tab-throttled / paused).

  * If the tab is hidden, `requestAnimationFrame` throttles; if you use ms timeouts, you can “timeout” while frames barely advance.

---

## If you’re using Phaser `snapshotArea` / `snapshotPixel`

### A) The “one snapshot per frame” rule (most common silent killer)

* Confirm you **never call any snapshot method twice in the same frame** anywhere in your code.
* Phaser explicitly: **only one snapshot can be active per frame; later calls override earlier ones**. ([docs.phaser.io][1])

**Hard check**: wrap `renderer.snapshot*` in debug builds and increment a counter per frame. If it’s ever > 1, you’ve found the bug.

### B) You schedule snapshot at a time Phaser will honor

* `snapshotArea` **schedules** a snapshot “after the current frame is rendered.” ([docs.phaser.io][1])
* Phaser has a renderer post-render event that fires **after rendering but before pending snapshots are taken**. ([docs.phaser.io][3])
  So:

  * **Do not schedule** new snapshots from “too-late” hooks where Phaser has already passed the snapshot step.
  * Safe scheduling times are typically during update/postupdate *before* render completes.

**Hard check**: log the phase where you call `snapshotArea` (update/postupdate/postrender). If you’re calling it in a post-render hook, that’s suspicious.

### C) Callback can be lost even when scheduled (your current symptom)

If `scheduled>0` but `callbacks=0`, check:

* You aren’t accidentally **overriding** the snapshot request later in the same frame (see A).
* Your callback doesn’t rely on `this` binding incorrectly (classic “callback loses context” issue). (Not your main symptom, but it breaks work inside the callback.) ([Phaser][4])
* You aren’t running into a browser-specific snapshot issue (Safari has known differences). ([Phaser][5])

### D) Canvas taint / CORS (can break “pixel readback” paths)

* If any texture is loaded without proper CORS headers and you draw it, the canvas can become **tainted** (then extracting pixels / ImageData can fail).
* This usually throws a **SecurityError** somewhere. Make sure you surface *all* thrown errors (wrap inside callback).

### E) Your timeout logic isn’t lying to you

* If your “timeout” is ms-based and the tab is throttled, you’ll timeout even though the snapshot would have eventually completed.
* Use a hybrid: timeout if **(ageMs > X AND ageFrames > Y)**.

---

## If you’re using **direct WebGL `gl.readPixels`** (recommended when snapshot callback is unreliable)

This is the “I need pixel truth and it must complete” path.

### 1) You call `readPixels` at the correct time

* Call it **after the frame is drawn**, e.g. Game Post-Render (end of render). ([docs.phaser.io][6])
* If you call it during update, you’ll read **last frame** or partially-drawn content.

**Hard check**: log once per second from your postrender handler. If it’s not firing, you’re not hooked correctly.

### 2) You read from the correct framebuffer

* Before `readPixels`, explicitly `gl.bindFramebuffer(gl.FRAMEBUFFER, null)` to read the default backbuffer (unless you intentionally render to an FBO).
* Restore prior binding afterward if you don’t control the whole renderer state.

**Hard check**: log `gl.getParameter(gl.FRAMEBUFFER_BINDING)` before/after.

### 3) You use drawingBuffer dimensions, not CSS / viewport dimensions

* Use `gl.drawingBufferWidth/Height` and map from viewport coords with scale factors. ([WebGL Fundamentals][2])
* Typical mapping:

  * `scaleX = drawingBufferWidth / renderer.width`
  * `scaleY = drawingBufferHeight / renderer.height`

### 4) You handle the origin flip

* `readPixels` is bottom-left origin. ([Stack Overflow][7])
* If you want “screen coordinates,” flip rows in the returned buffer (or map y accordingly).

### 5) Pack alignment

* Always `gl.pixelStorei(gl.PACK_ALIGNMENT, 1)` before `readPixels` to avoid row alignment surprises.

### 6) Context loss

* If the WebGL context is lost, readback becomes garbage or fails.
* Check `gl.isContextLost()` if available and fail fast.

### 7) Size and frequency

* `readPixels` is a GPU→CPU sync point. If you read large rectangles frequently, you can stall the frame loop and *create your own timeouts*.

  * Prefer: one rect per frame, small union rect, or sample points only.
  * If you need “19 samples,” don’t do 19 reads—do **one union read** and sample 19 points from that buffer.

---

## The “union read” rule (makes systems stable)

If you have multiple requests per frame:

* Compute `unionRect = bounds(allRequestedRects)`
* **One** `readPixels(unionRect)`
* For each sample point/rect, compute offset into the union buffer and sample from there.

This single decision eliminates:

* the snapshot “one per frame” override problem ([docs.phaser.io][1])
* excessive GPU stalls from many reads

---

## Minimal “it works” sanity test (do this before touching your real VFX)

1. Render a known solid rectangle at a known screen coordinate.
2. In postrender, `readPixels` a 4×4 area that includes the rect.
3. Assert one pixel is exactly the expected RGBA.

If this test fails, **stop**: the problem is timing/binding/coords—not your VFX system.

---

## Diagnostics you should always log in your dumps

If sampling is flaky, your dump must include:

* **Event/timing**

  * `lastPostrenderFrameSeen`
  * `lastCaptureFrame`
* **GL state**

  * `drawingBufferWidth/Height`
  * `FRAMEBUFFER_BINDING` at capture
  * `gl.isContextLost()`
* **Mapping**

  * `renderer.width/height`
  * `scaleX/scaleY`
  * requested rect, clipped rect, framebuffer rect
* **Counters**

  * requested, scheduled, captures, callbacks, completed, timeouts, errors
* **One snapshot per frame guard**

  * “snapshotCallsThisFrame”

---

## What your current evidence points to

Your diag says:

* scheduled snapshot calls happen,
* no external overrides,
* **callbacks never fire**.

That strongly suggests either:

* you’re scheduling at the wrong phase (too late), or
* snapshot pipeline is broken on your setup, so you should stop relying on it.

Given how much time you’ve already burned: **switch WebGL capture to postrender + `gl.readPixels` union rect**, keep Phaser snapshot only as a fallback / Canvas path. Snapshot internals literally use `readPixels` anyway. ([phaser.io][8])

---

1. the exact hook you’re using for capture (which event), and
2. the exact mapping code from `(screenX,screenY,screenW,screenH)` → `readPixels(x,y,w,h)`,

…I’ll tell you which checklist item you’re violating and give you the corrected full-function replacement.

effects-hall-trace was ~6,064,451 bytes
trace-proof was ~12,129,578 bytes
Server limit is currently 2 MB, so it returns reason=too-large.
I increased the server limit to 20 MB:

server.js → DEBUG_DUMP_MAX_BYTES = 20 * 1024 * 1024
Please restart the server again so the new limit applies, then run the trace. The dump should now succeed and write debugdump_traceproof.json.

It did write a file — and it’s not the normal debug dump.

What you have now is debugdump_traceproof.json, and it contains only:

type, createdAt, startMs, durationMs, intervalMs, alphaMin, samples
So it’s the raw effects-hall trace payload, not the full heDebugDumpV1. That’s expected because the direct send at trace completion is:

net.sendDebugDump(payload, "effects-hall-trace")
…and the server now routes that reason to debugdump_traceproof.json.

So yes: the trace did write, but it wrote the trace payload only, not the full debug dump. That’s why debugdump_latest.json doesn’t show effectsHallTrace.

Those “orphan” messages are tilemap debug dump responses arriving after their original timeouts. That’s why they all say file=debugdump_tilemap.json. They’re unrelated to the effects trace but they do load the server, which can delay the trace dump response.

I’ve now blocked tilemap visual dumps while the effects‑hall trace is active or in the trace‑proof hold window, so they won’t spam during tracing:

HeroEngineInPhaser.ts
_heVisualDumpTick now returns early if trace is active
_heQueueVisualDump now refuses to queue during trace/hold
This should stop the tilemap dump flood and let the trace dump respond before timeout.

DEBUG_TILEMAP_AUDIT_DUMP = true (this is what was generating tilemap dumps)
DEBUG_EFFECTS_HALL_LOGS = true

Lite screen sampling

YES YOU GOT IT!!! YOU SEE THE ISSUE!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!! Fantastic work!!!

Found the root cause in debugdump_traceproof.json: all four dust fills are being masked by the same mask sprite (hall/dust/cluster/3|mask, id 16). That’s why the medium fill becomes a square — it’s using the front mask instead of its own. The culprit is the occlusion block that overwrote the mask ref for every |fill.

I changed that block so it only applies to outlines, not fills. Fills now keep their own masks and should crop correctly.

Change:

HeroEngineInPhaser.ts — in the “Ensure back layers are occluded by the front layer” block, I now only apply the front mask to |mask-outline / |mask2-outline, not |fill.
Please rerun the effects hall trace and send the new debugdump_traceproof.json (or paste the updated effectsHallTrace.samples[0].items section). I’m specifically looking for each cluster’s fill.maskRefId to match its own mask tag (cluster 0→mask 0, cluster 1→mask 1, etc.). That should eliminate the square on the medium fill.

If you still need the back-layer fills to be occluded by the front layer and keep their own mask, we’ll need a composite mask (own mask AND NOT front mask). I can implement that next once we verify this fix.

I asked ChatGPT for help, can you confirm we are moving in this direction? OR clarify why we aren't?
Yep. This is the moment to **stop thinking “one sprite”** and instead lock a **scene-graph contract** so each concern has exactly one place to live. Then adding 4 clouds doesn’t blow up — it’s copy/paste of the same node stack.

Here’s the structure that keeps all 20 things straight.

---

## One cloud = one “CloudRig” with 3 layers

### 1) **CloudRig** (Container) — world placement + occlusion order

* Position: x/y
* Depth: the thing you sort to make clouds occlude each other
* **No rotation** (this is important)

You will have **four CloudRigs** with different depth / y-sorting.

---

### 2) **GrowRoot** (Container inside CloudRig) — growth axis (world-up)

* Handles your “growing upward” stretch: `scaleY`
* Pivot: bottom-anchored (so growth goes up)
* **No rotation**

This is what prevents “rotating makes it stretch sideways.”

---

### 3) **SpinRoot** (Container inside GrowRoot) — rotation only

* Handles rotation for variation: `rotation += …`
* This rotation should affect:

  * the *smoke border animation*
  * the *mask geometry*
* It should **NOT** affect the texture fill UVs (more on that below)

---

## Then inside SpinRoot you render **two visuals** that must line up

### A) Border sprite (visible)

* Animated smoke border (shows the silhouette)
* Rotates (because it’s inside SpinRoot)
* Purely visual; helps sell motion

### B) Mask sprite (invisible, used as BitmapMask)

* Same animation frames as border
* Rotates (inside SpinRoot)
* You don’t show it; it’s just the mask source

---

## The fill that must NOT rotate

This is the tricky part: if you attach the fill sprite under SpinRoot, it will rotate. You don’t want that.

So you **do not** rotate the fill object.

### Correct approach

Put the fill under **GrowRoot** (so it stretches upward with the cloud if you want that), but **not** under SpinRoot.

* Fill sprite/image:

  * sits under GrowRoot
  * does NOT rotate
  * gets masked by the mask sprite that *does* rotate

In Phaser, a BitmapMask can be created from a sprite anywhere; it doesn’t have to be its parent. So you can do:

* `mask = maskSprite.createBitmapMask()`
* `fillSprite.setMask(mask)`

That gives:

* border rotates + animates ✅
* mask rotates + animates ✅
* fill stays unrotated ✅
* fill still gets clipped by the rotating mask ✅

If your fill is a directional texture and you want it “stable in screen space,” keep fill rotation at 0 and only move/scale it.

---

## Occlusion between the 4 clouds

You want clouds to block each other. The simplest robust rule:

### Use depth sorting at the CloudRig level

* Each CloudRig gets a base depth.
* Within a CloudRig, you keep consistent internal order:

  * fill depth = 0
  * border depth = 1 (so you always see the animated edge on top of its own fill)

Then you set the rig’s overall depth so the whole cloud sits in front/behind another cloud.

**Practical rule that looks good:** sort by y (lower on screen is in front)

* `cloudRig.setDepth(cloudRig.y)` (or a scaled version)

If you need manual control, add an offset:

* `depth = y + bias`

---

## The one “don’t blow up” checklist

When you add a cloud, confirm these invariants:

1. **Only GrowRoot scales.**
2. **Only SpinRoot rotates.**
3. **Fill is NOT a child of SpinRoot.**
4. Mask sprite IS a child of SpinRoot.
5. Fill uses mask from mask sprite (bitmap mask).
6. Depth order is:

   * inside rig: fill behind border
   * between rigs: sort by depth/y

If those six hold, adding clouds doesn’t create weird axis bugs.

---

## One more gotcha (common)

If you scale GrowRoot but the mask sprite is inside it, the mask scales too — that’s usually fine (it keeps alignment).
If you ever need the mask NOT to scale (rare), you’d separate that too, but don’t unless you hit a specific artifact.

---

If you want, paste (or describe) how you currently create one cloud (what objects you spawn). I’ll map it into this CloudRig structure in a “rename these nodes / move these lines” way without rewriting your whole system.

You asked whether we’re moving in the ChatGPT “CloudRig/containers” direction or why we aren’t. Short answer: we’re matching the intent (separate fill vs. rotating mask, bottom‑anchored growth, per‑cloud occlusion), but not using Phaser Containers because the current pipeline is data‑driven + native effect glue, not scene‑graph containers.

Here’s how it maps:

What we are aligned with

Fill not rotating: fill sprites keep rotation=0; only mask sprites rotate (maskRotateSpeed / base).
Mask drives visibility: fills are masked by mask sprite (via EFFECT_MASK ref + bitmap mask on native).
Bottom‑anchored scaling: EFFECT_ALIGN_BOTTOM_Y keeps base fixed while scaling.
Occlusion between clouds: we’re building a composite occlusion mask for each back cloud from front masks. That’s beyond depth‑sorting and is required to stop borders from showing through.
What we’re not doing (and why)

No Phaser Containers (“CloudRig/GrowRoot/SpinRoot”): the current system uses arcadeCompat + effectAnimGlue to manage “native” sprites with data keys. Containers would require a sizable refactor and may conflict with the effect pipeline (which expects sprite data, not scene‑graph transforms).
Depth sorting alone isn’t enough: for your requirement (“borders/fills must be occluded even with transparency”), you need per‑pixel masks — container depth alone won’t block the border silhouette through a semi‑transparent front cloud.
So: yes, we’re moving in the right direction, but we’re implementing it in the current effect pipeline instead of rewriting into a container rig.

If you want to switch to the container‑rig architecture, I can do that — but it’s a bigger rework and may require rebuilding how effect sprites are synchronized with the native/arcade layer.
If you want to stay on the current path, I’ll continue tightening the occlusion masks and logs until borders/fills are fully blocked by front clouds.

Which route do you want?

Also: I already slowed the roil (maskFpsMult ~0.5–0.6). If it’s still too fast, I’ll lower the dust mask fps defaults globally.

What you need now is to make the system *behave like a rig* even if it’s “data + glue.”

---

## The mental model to keep everything straight (even without Containers)

Treat every cloud as having **three coordinate spaces** and never mix responsibilities:

### 1) WORLD space (placement + depth/ordering)

* x/y position
* “which cloud is in front”
* occlusion stacking order

### 2) GROW space (stretch up only, axis must not rotate)

* bottom-anchored scaleY growth
* **rotation MUST be 0 here**

### 3) SPIN space (rotation + animation only)

* the smoke border + mask rotate here
* the fill does **not** rotate here

Even if you’re not using containers, you can still enforce this by “who gets which transform values.”

---

## The rule set (the part that prevents blow-ups)

Per cloud, you have these objects:

* `fill` (texture)
* `mask` (animated alpha, invisible)
* `border` (animated outline, visible)

**Hard rules:**

1. `fill.rotation = 0` always. (Texture never rotates.)
2. `mask.rotation = spinAngle` and `border.rotation = spinAngle`. (They rotate together.)
3. Growth (scaleY) is applied in a way that does **not** rotate the growth axis:

   * Either scale the mask+border+fill uniformly in Y **but do not rotate the thing holding the scaling**, OR
   * Keep their rotations, but apply growth by adjusting their positions/scale around a fixed bottom anchor (the “align bottom” trick Codex mentioned).
4. Occlusion is handled by **a cumulative occluder mask**, front → back:

   * Back cloud visibility = backAlpha * (1 - occluderAlpha).

That last line is the missing “keep it sane” piece.

---

## Why depth sorting didn’t solve it (and why Codex is right)

Depth sorting only says “draw A after B.”
But if the front cloud is semi-transparent, you still see the back cloud through it (especially borders). You explicitly want **hard occlusion** behavior (“front blocks back”), which requires:

**backFinalAlpha = backAlpha × (1 − frontOcclusionAlpha)**

So Codex’s plan (“composite occlusion mask for each back cloud from front masks”) is exactly what you need.

---

## The one thing to add so it doesn’t keep derailing: assertions

Add a debug assert pass per frame (or when spawning) that checks:

* `fill.rotation == 0`
* `abs(mask.rotation - border.rotation) < tinyEps`
* `mask.frame == border.frame` (or intended offset)
* `fill.maskSpriteId == mask.id`
* occlusion: for back clouds, verify they’re using the current `occluderMaskId` (the composite of all clouds in front)

And include one visual debug toggle:

* show the current `occluderAlpha` as a grayscale overlay

That prevents “we’re looking at the wrong sprite” and “mask is right but not applied” spirals.

---

## What to tell Codex (so it stops asking “which route”)

**Route: stay current pipeline.**
Ask for these concrete next steps:

1. Keep the “virtual rig” contract:

   * growth via bottom align (no rotation involvement)
   * spin only on mask/border
   * fill never rotates

2. Implement occlusion as a running composite:

   * `occ = saturatingUnion(occ, frontMaskAlpha)` (or max/over)
   * `backVisible = backAlpha * (1 - occ)`

3. Apply occlusion to BOTH:

   * the back cloud fill
   * and the back cloud border (so borders don’t “ghost through”)

That’s it.

### The one missing spec to add

* i.e., use a thresholded/processed alpha (your floor / softknee output), possibly with a cutoff, so the occluder is stable and doesn’t flicker.

If you want “front blocks back” as a rule, **max** is usually the cleanest and least surprising.
