Now we have occlusion, but again--no visible borders. So STOP WHATEVER THE HELL YOU ARE DOING AND SELF ASSESS WHY YOU ARE GOING IN CIRCLES. What progress have we made?

You’re right to call this out. Here’s the self‑assessment and where we actually are:

What progress did happen

Occlusion is working for fills (front clouds are properly blocking back clouds).
The mask override path is working (we can route a cloud’s mask through a different sprite and it actually occludes).
Why it’s going in circles

We currently only have one mask per sprite.
When I route borders through the occluder mask, the border disappears because that occluder mask is built from geometry pixels using a threshold that does not include the outline region. So the mask is effectively “too small” and it eats the border.
When I route borders through the original mask, the border renders — but then no occlusion happens.
So the loop has been:
occlusion on → no border,
border on → no occlusion.

That’s not you; that’s the actual limitation of the current single-mask setup.

The correct next step
We need a border‑specific occluder, built from outline/edge geometry, not from the inner fill geometry:

Plan:

Keep the current occluder for fills (geometry, hard occlusion).
Generate a second occluder just for borders using a lower alpha threshold or by sampling the outline skin instead of the base geometry.
Apply that border‑occluder only to border sprites (fills keep the current occluder).
That preserves:

border visibility
border occlusion
fill occlusion
all at the same time.
If you want me to implement that, say the word and I’ll do it immediately.


Can we do something with using the OTHER borders to occlude the borders beneath them? Or is that a worse system? Because my objective is layering--so how do we get this layering?

Short answer: using other borders to occlude borders beneath them is worse, because borders are only a 1‑px ring. They won’t cover the overlap area of the front cloud, so the back border still “ghosts” through wherever the front border doesn’t exist. That fails your layering = hard occlusion rule.

What you actually need for correct layering:

Occlude borders with front cloud geometry, not with front borders.
That means: border visibility = outline of back cloud AND NOT covered by any front cloud geometry.
Constraint we’re hitting:

Phaser gives one mask per sprite, so we can’t do “outline mask AND occluder mask” in one step unless we pre‑compose the border into a new bitmap each update.
So the clean solution is:

Border Occlusion Path (correct)

Build a front‑geometry occluder (you already have this for fills).
Build a border image from the back cloud’s outline (as now).
Generate a border‑occluded image:
borderFinalAlpha = outlineAlpha AND NOT(frontOccAlpha)
Use that border‑occluded image as the border sprite’s texture.
This preserves:

visible border
border occlusion
no ghosting
true layering
If you want, I’ll implement this border‑occlusion image path next.
Tell me “go” and confirm the occluder threshold you want for geometry (e.g., 12).

Can you clarify something for me: What is "TileSprites"? What did you infer from me seaying we should tile these textures?

TileSprite is a Phaser object that draws one texture repeated (tiled) to fill a rectangular region. It keeps the texture seamless and lets it animate without stretching.

When you said “we should be tiling the texture since it seamlessly tiles,” I inferred you want the fill to be a repeated texture across the cloud area, not a single stretched image. That maps directly to TileSprite in Phaser (the engine’s built‑in tiling primitive).

Got it — discussion only.

You’re seeing gaps because the mask shape is changing (geometry frame changes + dust-rise scaling) while the fill surface (tiles + mask) isn’t perfectly in lockstep. There are a few non‑destructive tricks to hide or eliminate gaps without re‑architecting:

1) Slight “overfill” scale (safe, simplest)

Scale the fill (not the mask) up by a small factor (e.g. +2–4%) so it always covers the moving edge.
This doesn’t change occlusion logic; it just keeps the fill from undershooting when the mask changes.
Risk: if too large, it will peek beyond the mask when occlusion is off.
2) Mask pad‑out (geometry expansion)

Inflate the mask slightly before applying it to the fill (think morphological “grow” by 1–2px).
This keeps the fill visible right up to the edge, even if the mask shifts by a pixel between frames.
Risk: you lose crisp edges if you expand too much.
3) Sample‑then‑hold frame alignment

When the mask frame changes, delay the fill’s frame change by 1 tick (or vice‑versa) so the two don’t change on the same update.
This reduces temporal mismatch when both are animating.
Risk: if the animation is slow/noticeable, you might see a 1‑frame “drag.”
4) Scale on bottom‑anchor vs center‑anchor

If the fill is centered and the mask is bottom‑anchored, vertical scaling produces a visible mismatch.
Force both to bottom‑anchor scale (or compute fill y from mask bottom each tick).
Risk: if your current code doesn’t apply bottom anchor uniformly, this takes a bit more tuning.
5) Occlusion update on mask frame change only

For the occlusion composite: update only when the mask frame changes, not on a fixed timer.
This ensures occlusion never lags behind the visible shape (no gaps on motion).
Risk: if there’s heavy CPU, you’ll pay when frames are fast.
6) Alpha floor for mask

Clamp mask alpha up so thin semi‑transparent edges don’t “puncture” the fill.
This was already in play with your maskAlphaMinClamp values, but increasing those slightly reduces gaps.
Risk: the silhouette becomes chunkier if too high.
If you want the least invasive option: I’d try (1) slight fill scale or (2) 1–2px mask pad‑out first. They’re purely visual and don’t rewire the logic.

Tell me which approach you want to try and how aggressive you want it (e.g., +2% fill scale, or 1px pad‑out).


Is there any way to "capture" the old frame or something temporaily to do temporal tricks? I.e., it's not a problem if they aren't in lockstep because we have 1 frame behind "saved" so there is never a visible gap because the "old" frame is there fading away? Again--DISCUSSION. NOT implementation

Yes, there are a few temporal tricks you can use to hide gaps without forcing perfect lockstep. Conceptually, you keep the previous frame’s mask/fill around for a short time and blend it with the current frame so there’s no “hole” even if frames drift.

Here are the practical variants:

1) Two‑frame mask blend (temporal union)

Keep last frame mask and current frame mask.
Use max(oldMask, newMask) as the mask for a single frame (or for 1–2 frames).
That guarantees the new frame can only expand visibility, never shrink to a hole.
Tradeoff: you get a tiny “afterimage” on shrink phases, but it looks like lingering smoke (often good).
2) Double‑buffered fill (cross‑fade)

Render two fills: current and previous.
Fade previous down over ~50–150ms.
Mask each with its own mask frame.
Tradeoff: extra draw cost; can cause “ghosting” if too slow.
3) Sticky edge rule

Maintain a 1–2px dilated mask from the previous frame and union it with the current frame.
Similar to #1 but only “sticks” the edge, not the whole silhouette.
Tradeoff: subtle blur on edges, but minimal ghosting.
4) Mask hysteresis

When alpha drops below threshold, don’t remove it immediately — keep it for N frames.
This works like temporal smoothing without storing full frames.
Tradeoff: you can get a slight “laggy” edge on rapid shrink.
5) “Hold‑last” on occlusion only

If the gap is caused by occlusion lag, you can keep the previous occluder for 1 frame and union it with the new occluder.
Tradeoff: back layers may be occluded for one extra frame (usually fine).
These are all realistic. If you want something that feels cheap but effective, I’d recommend #1 (two‑frame union for mask) or #5 (occluder union). Both are visually stable and look like smoke “lingers.”

If you want, I can outline how to integrate one of these without major refactors.


Can you rank these by order of complexity/risk of fucking up my FINALLY SUCCESSFUL AFTER AN ENTIRE WEEK SPENT ON THIS STUPID FUCKING DUST

Because I DO NOT want to revert

Here’s the ranking from lowest risk / least invasive to highest risk / most likely to destabilize:

Occluder union (previous + current) for 1 frame

Touches only the occlusion composite.
Doesn’t change fill/mask wiring.
Risk: tiny 1‑frame “extra occlusion,” usually harmless.
Two‑frame mask union (previous + current)

Operates on the mask itself, still one output mask.
Risk: mild “afterimage” on shrink; generally looks like lingering smoke.
Sticky edge (dilate previous frame by 1px, then union)

Slightly more logic and edge handling.
Risk: subtle edge softness; still stable.
Mask hysteresis (keep pixels for N frames)

Requires per‑pixel state or cached mask frames.
Risk: visible lag on rapid changes.
Double‑buffered fill (previous fill fading out)

Adds another visible layer; heavier and easier to mis‑align.
Risk: ghosting + performance hit if not tuned.
Given your “do not revert” constraint, I’d recommend (1) occluder union first or (2) mask union second. Both are minimal and low blast‑radius.

If you want, we can plan #1 in more detail without committing code.


For 1, I think you misunderstand--we are occluding TOO much, that is why the gap is visible. So why would occluding MORE be the answer?

You’re right — if the occluder is already too aggressive, unioning more occluder makes it worse. That fix only helps when occlusion is lagging behind (under‑occluding). Your symptom is the opposite: occlusion removes back, but the front fill isn’t covering yet, so you see holes.

So the real choice is which rule you want:

A) Hard occlusion (front always blocks back)
Then you must guarantee front has visible color everywhere the occluder says it blocks. Options:

Force fill alpha floor to ~1 (or very high) within the occluder.
Or add a base color layer masked by the same occluder (not behind the fill), so holes never show floor.
B) Occlusion bounded by fill (no holes, but transparent front lets back show)
Then occluder should be derived from the fill mask, not raw geometry.
That guarantees “no gap,” but sacrifices hard blocking when the front is transparent.

If you want A, the fix is to make the front always have a body (base color or fully opaque fill).
If you want B, the fix is to make occlusion follow the fill visibility, not geometry.

Which rule do you want to enforce?


This is not A versus B. Because there are other options for SOLUTIONS

Agreed. The A/B framing is too narrow. Here are other solution paths that keep hard occlusion and avoid gaps, ordered by “least invasive”:

Temporal hold of the front fill
Keep the previous fill mask (or fill sprite) for 1–2 frames and union it with the current fill.
Result: occlusion stays hard, but you never see a hole during rapid shape changes.

Occlusion from geometry, but “color from fill”
Use geometry for occlusion, but sample the fill’s color at those occluded pixels (or a cached fill color).
Result: hard occlusion, but gaps are filled with a color that matches the fill.

Edge‑only fallback
If the front occluder is present but the front fill alpha < threshold at that pixel, render a thin edge tint (1–2px) instead of empty.
Result: no obvious holes, minimal extra tinting.

Staggered update ordering
Force fill update to occur before occlusion update each tick.
Result: occlusion never references a “future” geometry state that the fill hasn’t reached.

Clamp fill alpha to a floor only during occlusion
For pixels that are about to occlude back layers, raise fill alpha just for those pixels.
Result: hard occlusion without full opacity everywhere.

Those are all valid and don’t require disabling occlusion or re‑wiring the whole pipeline.

If you want, pick one or two and we’ll spec it precisely (no code changes until you approve).


Can we do 1 frame ahead and 1 frame behind for the texture mask such that it GAINS size? Becaus what is happpening is that the geometry rotates (intentionally). So if we can make the texture fill use 3 frames it will cover forwards/backwards/current and simply "gain" size. And that size will be negligible and if anything IMPROVE aesthetics. Is that possible? I mean WITHOUT trying to use 3 masks per fill

Yes, it’s possible without “three masks per fill,” but it requires one composite mask that merges multiple frames.

Conceptually:

You keep one mask object for the fill.
Internally, that mask’s texture is generated by combining frame (t‑1), frame (t), frame (t+1) into a single alpha mask (e.g., max(a_prev, a_cur, a_next)).
The fill still uses one BitmapMask; it just points at this composited mask texture.
That gives you the “gains size” effect (it’s a superset of the three frames), which is exactly what you want for rotation gaps.

Tradeoffs:

Slightly thicker silhouette (usually good for smoke).
Small CPU cost to build the combined mask each update.
Still respects hard occlusion, because the occluder can be the same union mask.
If you want, I can outline how to do this with minimal changes to the existing mask‑composite path (basically: extend the current union code to sample 3 frames for a single sprite instead of 1).


But how is this going to differentiate between the "occluding" mask and the "texture fill" mask? Because are those different? the same?

They should be different if you want both “no gaps in the fill” and “hard occlusion.”

Think of it as two roles:

Fill mask = “where the texture is allowed to show”

This is the one you want to grow using (t‑1, t, t+1) union, so you never see holes when the geometry rotates.
Occluder mask = “where the front blocks the back”

This should stay geometry‑accurate (often just current frame, maybe thresholded), otherwise you’ll over‑occlude.
So: same source sheet, different composites.
They can be built from the same three frames, but processed differently:

Fill mask: max(prev, cur, next) (maybe with alpha floor)
Occluder: threshold(cur) (or threshold(max(prev,cur,next)) if you want extra safety)
If you make them the same, you’re trading off: the fill will be gap‑free but the occluder will be too “fat,” or the occluder will be correct but the fill will still gap.

If you want, we can define the exact rule set (which frames for each, what alpha threshold).


But what is our CURRENT system?

Current system (as of the last code I saw):

Fill mask and occluder are the same mask.
When a cloud has an occlusion composite, we set maskFx.__heMaskNativeOverride = occ.maskImage, and every dependent sprite (fills, borders, tiles) uses that override as its mask source.

Occluder is built from geometry only (current frame, thresholded).
That same geometry‑based mask is then also what the fill uses, so any gap in geometry = gap in fill.

So today there is no separation between “fill mask” and “occlusion mask.” They’re effectively the same object once occlusion is active.

If you want them differentiated, the next step is to add a second composite (fill‑mask union) and keep occlusion strictly geometry‑thresholded.


I DO NOT want them differentiated. What I am saying is "if we do my solution of holding frames how will this actually increase texture fill coverage without amplifying the occlusion"

Understood. With the current system, fill and occlusion share the same mask image, and occlusion treats any non‑zero alpha as blocking because HALL_MASK_OCCLUDE_ALPHA_MIN = 1.
So: if we union prev/cur/next at full alpha, occlusion will expand. There’s no way around that if the threshold is 1.

However, there is a way to keep one mask and still get “fill expands, occlusion doesn’t”:

Use weighted prev/next + raise occlusion threshold
Compose mask as:
a = max(cur, k*prev, k*next)
where k is small enough to stay below the occlusion threshold.
Raise HALL_MASK_OCCLUDE_ALPHA_MIN above those k‑values.
Result: fill grows (because it uses alpha directly), occlusion stays the same (because prev/next never exceed the threshold).
Example in plain terms:

Set occlusion threshold to ~16 (out of 255).
Add prev/next at ~8–12 alpha (k ≈ 0.03–0.05).
Current frame is still full alpha and occludes normally.
Prev/next only help the fill.
That keeps a single mask, avoids the occlusion “balloon,” and fills gaps.

If you don’t want to raise the occlusion threshold, then any temporal union will necessarily expand occlusion — there’s no math trick around that with a shared mask.

So the decision is:

Keep threshold = 1 → union will amplify occlusion
Raise threshold + low‑alpha prev/next → fill expands, occlusion stays same
If you want, I can propose specific numbers based on the alpha range in your smoke frames (no code yet).


But we need to ensure CURRENT occlusion remains COMPLETELY untouched. ALL That should happen is we get the texture fill covering the gaps caused by the occluder being not perfectly in sync

Short answer: with a single shared mask and “occlusion = any alpha > 0,” you cannot increase fill coverage without also increasing occlusion.
They are the same mask, so any expansion that makes the fill visible in new pixels will also cause those pixels to occlude.

If “occlusion completely untouched” means pixel‑for‑pixel identical to now, then the only options left are:

Perfect lockstep (no temporal expansion)

Ensure occlusion composite and fill mask update on the same frame so you don’t see transient gaps.
This doesn’t increase coverage; it only removes mismatches.
Raise the geometry alpha floor

Makes the mask less transparent → fewer gaps.
But this does change occlusion, because the mask gets thicker.
If you’re willing to allow minimal occlusion change (only at tiny alpha values), then the temporal‑union trick can work: give prev/next frames a very low alpha and raise the occlusion threshold above that. That keeps the occlusion silhouette basically the same while letting the fill get a tiny “buffer.”

So the decision is:

Strictly no occlusion change → only lockstep synchronization is valid.
Allow a tiny, controlled occlusion change → temporal union with thresholding works.
Tell me which constraint is actually non‑negotiable (pixel‑exact occlusion vs “no visible occlusion change”), and I’ll map the safest path.


Didn't you already try 1? Why didn't it work before? What would you do differently?

We did try lockstep by removing the min update interval. It helped, but it didn’t eliminate gaps because the real mismatch is frame source and update order, not just timing.

Why it still failed:

The occlusion composite currently resolves frames using native.frame, which updates after render.
The mask/fill logic uses the effect data frame index (frame list + index) during update.
So occlusion is often one frame behind the geometry the fill is using, even if we update every frame.
What I’d do differently (discussion only):

Drive occlusion off the same frame index the mask uses, not native.frame.

Use EFFECT_FRAME_LIST_DATA_KEY + EFFECT_FRAME_INDEX_DATA_KEY to resolve the exact frame ID.
That guarantees occluder == mask frame.
Update occlusion on frame change, not time.

If the frame index changes, recompute occlusion immediately.
If it doesn’t, skip.
Run occlusion after mask frame update in the same tick.

So the occlusion always sees the frame that will be rendered this frame.
That keeps occlusion pixel‑identical to current geometry (no widening) but fixes the “frame behind” gaps.

If you want me to pursue this approach, I can outline the exact data dependencies so we don’t refactor or risk regressions.

Right — with a BitmapMask, anything outside the mask is clipped. So padding the fill size won’t help unless the mask itself grows.

To get “padding beyond the mask” you’d have to expand the mask, which is exactly what you said you don’t want because it changes occlusion. So the padding test proved the point.

Given that, the only gap‑free options that don’t expand the mask are:

Lockstep occlusion / mask frame (so the mask and fill are never out of sync).
Temporal union with low‑alpha prev/next and a higher occlusion threshold (so occlusion doesn’t expand but fill does).
Occlusion tied to fill visibility (no gaps, but less hard occlusion).
If you want to keep occlusion unchanged, the only viable path is (1) lockstep without relying on native frame timing — specifically, using the mask’s own frame index and updating occlusion on every mask frame change and every dust‑rise scale change (which we just did). That suggests the remaining gaps are not from time lag; they’re from mask geometry vs fill geometry mismatch.

If you want, I’ll walk through where that mismatch can happen (mask scale vs fill scale, bottom anchor mismatch, tile sampling origin). But the key: padding outside the mask is impossible by design unless you grow the mask.

What’s still not lockstep:

Tile fill doesn’t use the effect frame index at all; it advances on a separate timer. Even if the mask and occlusion are synced, the tile can still be a frame behind.
So the remaining lockstep gaps are likely from:

Occlusion uses native frame while mask uses effect frame.
Tile fill uses its own timer, not the mask’s frame index.
If you want true lockstep without expanding masks, the next steps are:

Drive occlusion from the same frame index as the mask (ignore native.frame).
Drive tile fill frame from the same frame index (or from mask tick), instead of its own timer.

## 5b) Dust Cloud Occlusion (Hall) — working recipe

This is the approach that finally made layered dust clouds work without
border leaks or mask drift. Use it as the template for future layered effects.

### Core idea
Each cloud is a **mask sprite + tiled fill**, and occlusion is a **separate
geometry-based composite** that blocks anything behind it.

### Required pieces
- **Geometry sheet:** `SmokeBorderedRotating grayscale 128x128`
  - The border is baked into the sheet; do not try to add a separate outline layer.
- **Fill texture:** `HALL_DUST_TEX_ID` (tile sprite, seamless)
- **Mask sprite:** the geometry sheet, hidden but still alpha-active
  - Set `__hallMaskKeepAlpha=1` so hidden masks keep alpha for masking.

### Occlusion (front blocks back, even if front is semi-transparent)
- Build a canvas occluder (`__hallMaskOcclude`) from **geometry alpha**, not visual alpha.
- Use `HALL_MASK_OCCLUDE_ALPHA_MIN` as the threshold.
- For each back cloud, apply `(1 - occluder)` to **both** fill and border.
- Do not gate occlusion on visibility; use the occlusion canvas mask directly.

### Lockstep (prevents gaps)
- Drive **all transforms** from effect data, not native sprite values:
  - `EFFECT_SCALE_DATA_KEY`, `EFFECT_SCALE_X_DATA_KEY`, `EFFECT_SCALE_Y_DATA_KEY`
  - `EFFECT_ROT_DATA_KEY`
  - `EFFECT_ALIGN_BOTTOM_Y_DATA_KEY`
- Apply bottom-anchoring to native sprites each tick so the visible mask,
  occlusion mask, and tile fill all share the same base line.

### Texture/scene access (avoid null scene)
- When resolving frames for occlusion, use `native.texture.manager` (or
  `native.scene`) rather than `globalThis.__phaserScene`.
  This avoids occlusion updates silently skipping.

### Debug tell-tales (from effects hall trace)
- `maskOcclude.lastUpdateAt > 0`  → occlusion composite is updating
- `tileOcclude.usesOcclude == 1` → tiles are actually using occlusion masks
- `nativeMaskSpriteId.textureKey` should be `effects.hallocclude.*`
- If `lastUpdateAt == 0`, occlusion is not running (usually scene/texture lookup)

## 6) REQUIRED Workflow for New Effects
Worked for 7m 15s
Kept the pipeline intact and applied the “both” lockstep pieces: occlusion now resolves frames via the native texture manager (so it actually updates), and bottom‑anchor alignment is enforced on the native sprite each tick. I also documented the working recipe in VFX_SYSTEM.md.

What I changed

HeroEngineInPhaser.ts: occlusion/union frame resolution no longer depends on globalThis.__phaserScene; it uses native.texture.manager (or native scene) so __hallOccludeUpdateAt actually advances.
arcadeCompat.ts: if EFFECT_ALIGN_BOTTOM_Y_DATA_KEY is set, native y is bottom‑anchored after animation glue, keeping mask/fill/occluder locked together.
VFX_SYSTEM.md: added “Dust Cloud Occlusion (Hall) — working recipe” section.
Why this is surgical (based on the dump)
Your latest traceproof dump shows:

maskOcclude.lastUpdateAt == 0
tileOcclude.usesOcclude == 0
That means occlusion never updates and tiles never switch to the occlusion mask. The change above targets that exact failure (scene/texture lookup), without touching any of the working mask/alpha logic.

Review
OK great. EVerything is working wonderfully. Now, I want to make some changes to the animation--I need these to read actual "clouds of dust from the architecture lowering into the ground". How do we get there? We have all the eingredients here, all the tools. My "scaling" was purely to be able to raise the smoke up and cover the sprites and then have it lower and the sprites are disappearing into the smoke.

But visually it's not "just right" yet. I think part of that is the small/medium/large need to actually rise. I also think part of it is the scaling is just a cop out for a true "the smoke cloud is rumbling and building and rising out of the earth and then going back into the earth". So I want IDEAS

Here are concrete, low‑risk ideas to make it read as “dust welling up from the ground, rumbling, then sinking,” without touching the occlusion system.

Lowest risk (pure timing/pose changes)

Add vertical drift per cluster: small/medium/large rise a bit (e.g., 6–14px) while the base of the central stays pinned. This makes the plume feel like it’s lifting off the floor instead of just stretching.
Ease in/out, not linear: use a slow‑start, fast‑middle, slow‑end curve for the rise and fall (e.g., easeOutCubic up, easeInCubic down). That reads like pressure building then collapsing.
Stagger phase subtly (not direction): keep all clouds rising/falling together, but offset their phase by a few frames so edges “rumble.” You already do this for frame indices; do the same for vertical offset (±5% of the rise timeline).
Medium risk (still within current pipeline)

Add a “ground‑hug” stage: first 10–15% of the cycle keeps height almost flat while opacity increases, then it rises. This creates the sense of dust pushing up before it expands.
Height vs width decouple: allow height to rise more than width early, then let width catch up late. This reads like a plume instead of a blob.
Two‑stage fall: quick collapse to ~60% height, then slower sink to minimum. Feels like dust settling.
Higher risk (optional, but can look great)

Micro‑swirl displacement: a tiny lateral jitter (1–3px) on the small/medium clouds while rising. Adds “roil” without breaking occlusion.
Edge agitation: increase mask FPS slightly only during the peak (e.g., 20% of cycle) to make the edges rumble when the plume is strongest.
Add a thin base haze layer: a very low‑alpha, wide tile fill that stays near the ground. This helps sell “dust lingering on the floor” while the main cloud rises.
If you want, I’ll propose a specific timeline curve (numbers and durations) for the rise/fall cycle and how much vertical lift per cloud. The key is: small/medium/large should move upward (translation) while the central stays anchored, and the scaling should feel like expansion, not “lifting.”


Let us please begin with the lowest risk changes and let me see the effects (and ensure we don't lose functionality)

Fantastic. Now, I want to try actually USING this with the teleport platform and pillar. But I need strategy here. My original idea was to just make the smoke clouds tall enough to "hide" the pad and pillar. But I think that alone won't really work--we need the pad and pillar to "sink" where they visually move downwards on the map, but they are actually getting masked so as they lower down past X point they stop being visible. Thus when they have lowered completely neither will be visible and the dust clousd can disappear and the pad/pillar can be set to "hidden" since they will visually already BE hidden becaues they have lowered down fully into whatever will crop them.

How do we get that? I need ideas

Here’s a low‑risk, practical plan that builds on what already works:

Goal: pad + pillar visually sink into the ground while the dust hides the transition, and by the time dust fades out, the objects are already fully hidden.

Strategy (minimal new tech):
Use existing occlusion mask from the dust cloud as a “cutout” for the pad/pillar while you animate their Y downwards. No new shaders, no new pipelines.

Option A — Mask the pad/pillar with the dust occluder (best fidelity, medium effort)

When dust starts:
Record padStartY, pillarStartY.
Start dust rise/fall animation as you already do.
During the dust “rise” window:
Move pad/pillar downward (visual sink).
Apply bitmap mask to pad/pillar using the dust occluder mask (not the visual fill).
This ensures: once a part goes below the cloud’s opaque geometry, it disappears.
At the end (fade‑out second):
Set pad/pillar visible=false or alpha=0.
Remove masks to keep runtime clean.
Let dust fade.
This gives the most “true” visual: the dust geometry defines what is hidden, and the physical Y motion sells the sink.

Option B — Fake it with only Y motion + dust cover (lowest risk, less perfect)

Move pad/pillar down over 4–5 seconds.
Make dust tall enough to cover them as they sink.
When they’re fully below the dust, hide them.
No masking of pad/pillar.
This is simpler, but you’ll still see pad edges peeking through unless dust is overly thick.
Option C — “Cutout strip” mask at ground plane (very stable, simple)

No dust mask.
Create a static rectangular mask that hides everything below a fixed Y plane.
Move pad/pillar down through the plane.
Dust still plays above it.
This is robust and low‑risk but less “organic.”
Recommendation:
Start with Option A, because it uses the already working occlusion system, so you get real masking without new pipelines. If it’s too invasive, fall back to B.

If you want, I can draft the exact data flow for Option A using the current occluder setup (which mask to reuse, when to attach/detach, and how to keep it cheap).


With Option A though how do we make it so that after the pad/pillar move BELOW The dust cloud they don't show again? That option doesn't sound good to me because it relies too much on the smoke

Good catch. Option A only “hides while inside the mask.” If the pad/pillar sink below the dust and the dust thins or moves, they can reappear. So you need a second, stable hide rule that takes over once they’re past a cutoff.

Two low‑risk ways to do that:

A1) Mask + Depth‑plane cutoff (recommended)

While dust is active: use dust occluder mask and animate Y down.
Once the pad/pillar passes a fixed “ground cutoff Y”:
flip a flag isSubmerged=true
set visible=false (or alpha=0) and leave it hidden regardless of dust.
When you bring it back later, you reset visible=true and start the reverse motion.
That gives you the dust‑driven “organic hide,” but guarantees no reappearance after it sinks past the threshold.

A2) Mask + Permanent clip plane

Keep a static rectangular clip/mask at the floor plane that hides anything below it.
Dust occluder handles the transition above the plane.
Once the object passes the plane, it’s permanently hidden by the plane mask (so it won’t pop back even if dust moves).
This is very stable but adds another mask object to manage.

If you want Option A to feel safe: do A1.
It’s one extra check and doesn’t add new rendering complexity. It just says: “below this Y, you’re gone.”

If that sounds right, tell me the cutoff you want (tile row, world Y, or “the pad’s base minus N px”) and I’ll wire it.

Worked for 57s
You’re right: if the hide rule depends on dust, it’s unstable.
To make sinking reliable, you need a ground‑plane occluder that is independent of the dust.

Lowest‑risk ways to do that:

1) Crop by a Y‑cutoff (recommended)
This is deterministic and doesn’t care about dust.

Define a cutoffY (the “ground plane”).
Each tick, compute how much of the pad/pillar is above that plane.
Use setCrop(...) to show only the portion above the plane.
Dust mask still applies on top for the transition.
Result: dust can be large or thin; the object never reappears below the plane.

This works best because pads/pillars don’t rotate. Crop is cheap and stable.

2) Plane mask + dust mask (more complex)
You’d build a composite mask = (above‑plane mask) AND (not‑dust occluder).
Phaser only allows one mask, so you’d have to pre‑compose those masks into a canvas and use that as the mask source.

Higher risk and more code; only needed if you must rotate the object.

3) Hard plane clipping only (dust purely visual)
If you don’t need the dust to actually occlude the pad/pillar, just use the plane crop. Dust is just a visual overlay and doesn’t affect the object.

If you want, I’ll implement Option 1:

per‑object crop by a fixed cutoffY
dust occluder still used to hide the transition above the plane
Let me know the cutoff reference you want (tile row, world Y, or “pad base minus N px”).

Yes — what you’re describing is exactly per‑pixel clipping by a fixed Y plane, not “hide now.” That’s achievable and stable.

Two clean ways to do it (both keep the top visible while the bottom sinks past the cutoff):

Option 1: Dynamic crop (lowest risk, very predictable)
Each tick:

Compute sprite topY / bottomY.
Compute how much of the sprite is above cutoffY.
Set setCrop(0, 0, frameW, cropH) where cropH maps to the visible height.
This gives you partial visibility as it sinks. It is independent of dust, so no reappearances.

Option 2: Geometry mask plane (cleaner, no per‑frame crop math)

Create a Graphics rectangle covering the “above ground” region.
Use it as a GeometryMask on the pad/pillar.
As the object sinks, the mask naturally clips everything below cutoffY.
This is simpler to maintain, but you only get one mask per object in Phaser.
If the pad/pillar doesn’t already need a bitmap mask, this is ideal.

Important note about dust occlusion
If you want the dust to also occlude the pad/pillar in an irregular way and you want the plane cutoff, that’s two masks, which Phaser doesn’t support directly. You’d need to pre‑compose them into a single mask (higher effort).

Dust remains visual only (and keeps its own occlusion for dust‑on‑dust)

a fixed world Y?
a tile row?
“top of the dust cloud at peak height”?

Yes — and in Phaser that “lazy square below” idea maps cleanly to a single plane‑cut. You don’t need the smoke to be the mask; it just hides the cut line.

Two simple versions:

Option A: Dynamic crop (simplest, no extra mask objects)
Each tick, compute how much of the object is above a fixed Y cutoff, then set crop height.

Why it fits your idea:
It’s literally “object sinks; after an offset, it starts getting cropped.”

Key math (conceptual):

topY = obj.y - obj.displayHeight * obj.originY
visibleH = clamp(cutoffY - topY, 0, obj.displayHeight)
obj.displayHeight * frameH
obj.setCrop(0, 0, frameW, cropH)
This is extremely stable and won’t re‑expose anything once it’s below the cutoff. Dust just hides the hard crop edge.

Option B: GeometryMask with a rectangle (still easy, one extra object)
Create a rectangle mask that covers “above ground.”

Place rectangle at a fixed cutoff Y (or cutoff + offset).
Apply it as a GeometryMask to the pad/pillar.
The object sinks; only the portion above the cutoff renders.
This is equivalent to your “lazy square,” but actually used as a mask.

Important constraint:
You only get one mask per object. If you ever want dust to also mask the pad, you’d have to pre‑compose masks (higher effort). Given your stated goal, a fixed cutoff is the right contract and dust is just the cover.

AGENTS.md instructions for /mnt/c/my-heroengine-phaser
<INSTRUCTIONS> # Agent Notes
This repository hosts a Phaser + HeroEngine integration. Keep debug/logging centralized and avoid noisy output by default.

Logging & Debug Flags
All log toggles live in debugFlags.ts.
Do not add console.* without a flag check; prefer existing flags first.
New flags must default to false and be documented in DEBUG_FLAGS.md.
Keep log tags consistent and bracketed (e.g., [SCOPE], [SCOPE][DETAIL]).
Prefer consolidated, copy-pasteable text logs: one combined entry that includes all needed fields (avoid multi-line or fragmented logs).
Collision Mask Rule (Ironclad)
For props/decor that collide, ALWAYS use the aura/mask collision data for close‑quarters collisions.
Never disable or bypass mask-based collisions; do not fall back to plain bounding boxes for colliding props.
Code Edits
Prefer rg for search.
Keep edits ASCII unless the file already uses non-ASCII.
Avoid touching assets/ or src/generated/ unless explicitly requested. If generation is needed, use the scripts in scripts/.
Keep changes scoped to the request; avoid refactors that aren’t asked for.
Student Folder Boundary (Ironclad)
For the maintainer and Codex: do NOT edit anything inside named student folders under src/student/<Name>/ after initial creation unless explicitly directed to do so.
Top-level repo docs (e.g., STUDENTS.md, STUDENT_SYSTEMS_GROUPS.md, copilot.md, DEBUG_FLAGS.md, AGENTS.md) are maintainer-owned and may be edited as needed.
The maintainer/Codex edit surface and the student/AI edit surface are intentionally separate and must never overlap.
Runs / Tests
Ask before running long or destructive commands.
Communication
If asking the user to re-verify something basic they likely already did, acknowledge that and explain why you need the confirmation.
Use phrasing like: "I understand you likely already did this, but for my algorithms to proceed could you please verify..." or "I know this is frustrating; to continue I need to confirm..."
When discussing debug flags, always open debugFlags.ts and reference the current value before asking the user to toggle or verify a flag.
Recency Rule (Critical)
Always prioritize the most recent user instructions over older context.
If an older instruction conflicts with a newer one, STOP and ask for clarification.
When resuming a long thread, explicitly restate the latest request before acting.
If the user says "no changes," do not modify any files until they say "go."
Asset Rules (Ironclad)
All asset PNG filenames MUST include their frame size as WxH (e.g., Book 32x40.png). No exceptions.
Never trim or auto-fix PNG dimensions at runtime. If a sheet is not an exact multiple of its frame size, HARD FAIL.
If asked to fix an asset, edit the ORIGINAL and create a backup copy in the repo before changing it.
Skills
A skill is a set of local instructions to follow that is stored in a SKILL.md file. Below is the list of skills that can be used. Each entry includes a name, description, and file path so you can open the source for full instructions when using a specific skill.

Available skills
skill-creator: Guide for creating effective skills. This skill should be used when users want to create a new skill (or update an existing skill) that extends Codex's capabilities with specialized knowledge, workflows, or tool integrations. (file: /home/krist/.codex/skills/.system/skill-creator/SKILL.md)
skill-installer: Install Codex skills into $CODEX_HOME/skills from a curated list or a GitHub repo path. Use when a user asks to list installable skills, install a curated skill, or install a skill from another repo (including private repos). (file: /home/krist/.codex/skills/.system/skill-installer/SKILL.md)
How to use skills
Discovery: The list above is the skills available in this session (name + description + file path). Skill bodies live on disk at the listed paths.
Trigger rules: If the user names a skill (with $SkillName or plain text) OR the task clearly matches a skill's description shown above, you must use that skill for that turn. Multiple mentions mean use them all. Do not carry skills across turns unless re-mentioned.
Missing/blocked: If a named skill isn't in the list or the path can't be read, say so briefly and continue with the best fallback.
How to use a skill (progressive disclosure):
After deciding to use a skill, open its SKILL.md. Read only enough to follow the workflow.
When SKILL.md references relative paths (e.g., foo.py), resolve them relative to the skill directory listed above first, and only consider other paths if needed.
If SKILL.md points to extra folders such as references/, load only the specific files needed for the request; don't bulk-load everything.
If scripts/ exist, prefer running or patching them instead of retyping large code blocks.
If assets/ or templates exist, reuse them instead of recreating from scratch.
Coordination and sequencing:
If multiple skills apply, choose the minimal set that covers the request and state the order you'll use them.
Announce which skill(s) you're using and why (one short line). If you skip an obvious skill, say why.
Context hygiene:
Keep context small: summarize long sections instead of pasting them; only load extra files when needed.
Avoid deep reference-chasing: prefer opening only files directly linked from SKILL.md unless you're blocked.
When variants exist (frameworks, providers, domains), pick only the relevant reference file(s) and note that choice.
Safety and fallback: If a skill can't be applied cleanly (missing files, unclear instructions), state the issue, pick the next-best approach, and continue. </INSTRUCTIONS>
